A combinatorial interpretation of the Bernstein degree of unitary highest weight modules
William Q. Erickson, Markus Hunziker

Abstract
. The Bernstein degree ( Deg \operatorname{Deg} ) is a fundamental invariant of admissible representations of a real reductive Lie group G ℝ G_{\mathbb{R}} . Our main result concerns the classical dual pairs ( G ℝ , H ℝ ​ ( k ) ) (G_{\mathbb{R}},H_{\mathbb{R}}(k)) , namely ( U ⁡ ( p , q ) , U ⁡ ( k ) ) (\U(p,q),\>\U(k)) , ( Mp ⁡ ( 2 ​ n , ℝ ) , O ⁡ ( k ) ) (\Mp(2n,\mathbb{R}),\>\operatorname{O}(k)) , and ( O ∗ ⁡ ( 2 ​ n ) , Sp ⁡ ( k ) ) (\operatorname{O}^{*}(2n),\>\Sp(k)) , where k k is any positive integer. In this setting, via Howe duality, each irreducible representation σ \sigma of H ℝ ​ ( k ) H_{\mathbb{R}}(k) corresponds to a unitary highest weight module L λ ⁡ ( σ ) L_{\lambda(\sigma)} for G ℝ G_{\mathbb{R}} . A landmark result of Nishiyama–Ochiai–Taniguchi (2001) expressed Deg ⁡ L λ ⁡ ( σ ) \operatorname{Deg}L_{\lambda(\sigma)} as a product of two quantities: the dimension of σ \sigma and the degree of the associated variety. However, this result was limited to a specific range of the parameter k k (namely k ≤ r k\leq r , the real rank of G ℝ G_{\mathbb{R}} ). The present paper resolves this limitation by introducing, for all k k , the combinatorial interpretation Deg ⁡ L λ ⁡ ( σ ) = # ⁡ ( 𝒬 k ​ ( σ ) × 𝒫 k ) \operatorname{Deg}L_{\lambda(\sigma)}=\#(\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)\times\mathcal{P}_{k}) , where 𝒬 k ​ ( σ ) \mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma) is a certain set of semistandard tableaux and 𝒫 k \mathcal{P}_{k} is a set of plane partitions. (The result remains partly conjectural in the Mp ⁡ ( 2 ​ n , ℝ ) \Mp(2n,\mathbb{R}) case.) Beyond the dual pair setting, we generalize the set 𝒫 k \mathcal{P}_{k} to all groups G ℝ G_{\mathbb{R}} of Hermitian type, and we exhibit analogues of the Nishiyama–Ochiai–Taniguchi result for certain families of unitary highest weight modules of E 6 \operatorname{E}_{6} and E 7 \operatorname{E}_{7} .



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 22E46; Secondary 05E10, 14L30, 32M15

1.
Introduction

1.1.
Bernstein degree of unitary highest weight modules

Let
X
be the
(\mathfrak{g},K)
-module of an admissible representation of a connected noncompact real reductive Lie group
G_{\mathbb{R}}
with finite center.
Two invariants arising from the asymptotic growth of a good
U(\mathfrak{g})
-filtration on
X
are its
Gelfand–Kirillov dimension

{\operatorname{Dim}}(X)
and its
Bernstein degree

\operatorname{Deg}X
.
By a result of Vogan
[
42
]
*Thm. 1.1, for any good filtration
\{X_{n}\}
,

\dim X_{n}\sim\frac{\operatorname{Deg}X}{{\operatorname{Dim}}(X)!}n^{{\operatorname{Dim}}(X)}

as
n\rightarrow\infty
.
Equivalently, there is a unique polynomial
Q_{X}(t)
with integer coefficients, such that the reduced Hilbert series of
X
is given by

(1.1)

H_{X}(t)=\frac{Q_{X}(t)}{(1-t)^{{\operatorname{Dim}}(X)}},\quad\text{and}\quad\operatorname{Deg}X=Q_{X}(1).

Uniting these two invariants is the notion of the associated variety
\mathcal{AV}(X)\subset\mathfrak{g}^{*}
, introduced by Vogan
[
42
,
43
]
.
In particular, we have
{\operatorname{Dim}}(X)=\dim\mathcal{AV}(X)
, while
\operatorname{Deg}X
encodes the multiplicity of
\mathcal{AV}(X)
as an algebraic cycle.

Harish-Chandra
[
20
,
21
]
showed that a noncompact simple Lie group
G_{\mathbb{R}}
admits nontrivial unitary highest weight modules if and only if
G_{\mathbb{R}}
is of Hermitian type, that is,
G_{\mathbb{R}}/K_{\mathbb{R}}
is a Hermitian symmetric space (where
K_{\mathbb{R}}
is a maximal compact subgroup).
From now on, suppose that
G_{\mathbb{R}}/K_{\mathbb{R}}
is an irreducible Hermitian symmetric space of noncompact type.
Let
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{R}}=\mathfrak{k}_{\mathbb{R}}\oplus\mathfrak{p}_{\mathbb{R}}
be the corresponding Cartan decomposition of
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{R}}
, and let
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{p}^{-}\oplus\mathfrak{k}\oplus\mathfrak{p}^{+}

be the triangular decomposition of its complexification.
The action of the complexified group
K
on the abelian nilradical
\mathfrak{p}^{+}
has finitely many orbits, whose closures form a chain of algebraic varieties

(1.2)

\{0\}=\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{0}\subset\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\subset\cdots\subset\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{r}=\mathfrak{p}^{+},

where
r
denotes the real rank of
G_{\mathbb{R}}
.

If
L
is an irreducible unitary highest weight module of
G_{\mathbb{R}}
, then its associated variety
\mathcal{AV}(L)
equals one of the orbit closures
\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{k}
in (
1.2
), and its associated cycle
[
44
]
takes the form

\mathcal{AC}(L)=m_{L}\,[\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{k}]

for some positive integer multiplicity
m_{L}
.
This yields the following expression for the Bernstein degree:

(1.3)

\operatorname{Deg}L=m_{L}\cdot\deg\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{k},

where
\deg
denotes the degree as an algebraic variety.

1.2.
The Nishiyama–Ochiai–Taniguchi result

The main result obtained by Nishiyama–Ochiai–Taniguchi in their paper
[
33
]
is an elegant formula for Bernstein degree in the following
dual pair
setting.
Given a positive integer
k
, consider one of the three pairs

(1.4)

(G_{\mathbb{R}},H_{\mathbb{R}}(k))=(\U(p,q),\>\U(k)),\quad(\operatorname{Mp}(2n,\mathbb{R}),\>\operatorname{O}(k)),\quad\text{or}\quad(\operatorname{O}^{*}(2n),\>\Sp(k)),

where
\operatorname{Mp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
denotes the metaplectic double cover of
\Sp(2n,\mathbb{R})
.
In each dual pair setting (
1.4
), let
\widehat{H}(k)
denote the set of irreducible representations of the compact group
H_{\mathbb{R}}(k)
(equivalently, of its complexification
H(k)
).
The irreducible representations
\sigma\in\widehat{H}(k)
parametrize a family of irreducible unitary highest weight modules of
G_{\mathbb{R}}
, in the sense that there is a map

(1.5)

\sigma\mapsto L_{\lambda(\sigma)}

where
L_{\lambda(\sigma)}
is the simple
\mathfrak{g}
-module with highest weight
\lambda(\sigma)
.
(See Table
2
for details.)
Upon restricting to those
\sigma
’s for which
L_{\lambda(\sigma)}\neq 0
, the map (
1.5
) is injective.
Moreover, for
G_{\mathbb{R}}=\U(p,q)
or
\operatorname{Mp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
, all irreducible unitary highest weight modules arise in this way as some
L_{\lambda(\sigma)}
.

For
\sigma\in\widehat{H}(k)
, it is known (see
[
12
]
*Thm. 6 or
[
27
]
) that
\mathcal{AV}(L_{\lambda(\sigma)})=\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{\min\{k,r\}}
, and hence by (
1.3
) there is some positive integer
m_{k}(\sigma)
such that

(1.6)

\operatorname{Deg}L_{\lambda(\sigma)}=m_{k}(\sigma)\cdot\deg\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{\min\{k,r\}}.

The main result of Nishiyama–Ochiai–Taniguchi
[
33
]
*Thm. B states that for sufficiently small
k
, this multiplicity
m_{k}(\sigma)
is given by the dimension of the irreducible representation
U_{\sigma}
of
H_{\mathbb{R}}(k)

labeled by
\sigma
:

(1.7)

\text{If $k\leq r$, then }\operatorname{Deg}L_{\lambda(\sigma)}=\dim U_{\sigma}\cdot\deg\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{k}.

1.3.
Main result of this paper

The Bernstein degree formula (
1.7
) is limited to the range
k\leq r
.
In this paper, we generalize (
1.7
) by introducing a uniform combinatorial interpretation that is valid for all
k
.
In particular, we replace
\dim U_{\sigma}
in (
1.7
) by the cardinality of a certain set
\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)
of semistandard tableaux,
and we replace
\deg\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{k}
by the cardinality of a set
\mathcal{P}_{k}
of plane partitions.
(See Definitions
3.1
and
4.1
.)
Our main result is the following theorem.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
G_{\mathbb{R}}
be one of the groups in the dual pair setting (
1.4
), where
k
is any positive integer.

(
In the
G_{\mathbb{R}}=\operatorname{Mp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
case, the result remains conjectural within a certain range of
k
-values; see details in Theorem
6.2
.
)

For all
\sigma\in\widehat{H}(k)
, we have

\operatorname{Deg}L_{\lambda(\sigma)}=\#\Big(\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)\times\mathcal{P}_{k}\Big).

In particular, the cardinality of
\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)
equals the multiplicity
m_{k}(\sigma)
in (
1.6
), and the tableaux in
\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)
satisfy certain constraints on the entries in their initial columns (see Definition
3.1
).
A key feature of our construction is that as
k
increases, these constraints on
\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)
progressively relax.
As a result, the sets
\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)
interpolate between linear bases of two distinguished representations:

(1.8)

\#\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)=\begin{cases}\dim U_{\sigma}&\text{if $k\leq r$},\\
\dim F_{\lambda(\sigma)}&\text{if $k\geq s$},\end{cases}

where
F_{\lambda(\sigma)}
is the simple
K
-module with highest weight
\lambda(\sigma)
, and where
s
is the threshold beyond which every
L_{\lambda(\sigma)}
is free as a
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{p}^{+}]
-module.
(See Table
2
.)
Indeed, when
k\leq r
, our set
\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)
(up to a constant shift in tableau entries) recovers a well-known tableau set modeling a basis for
U_{\sigma}
:
in the
\U(p,q)
case, this is the set of
rational tableaux
given by Stembridge
[
40
]
, and in the other two cases, this is the set of
orthogonal
or
symplectic
tableaux given by Proctor
[
35
]
.
On the other hand, when
k\geq s
, the initial column constraints on
\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)
vanish, meaning that the sets
\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)
become full sets of semistandard tableaux that model linear bases for irreducible representations of general linear groups (the group
K
in our setting).
In this way, our construction of
\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)
sheds light on the mysterious gap
r<k<s
left open in (
1.7
).

The cardinality of
\mathcal{P}_{k}
equals the degree of the orbit closure
\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{\min\{k,r\}}
in (
1.6
), and the plane partitions in
\mathcal{P}_{k}
are bounded by
k
and contained inside a certain diagram
D_{k}
(see Definition
4.1
).
In the dual pair setting,
\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{\min\{k,r\}}
is a determinantal variety inside a space of generic, symmetric, or skew-symmetric matrices (
7.7
);
consequently, this fact
\#\mathcal{P}_{k}=\deg\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{\min\{k,r\}}
is likely known to experts, due to the extensive work in the 1990s on determinantal rings and lattice paths.
(The bijection
\Theta
in Definition
4.4
connects the plane partition perspective with that of lattice paths.)
It is worth mentioning that the classical plane partition formulas for
\#\mathcal{P}_{k}
(see Proposition
4.2
) are much simpler than the original degree formulas due to Giambelli–Thom–Porteous
[
18
,
22
,
34
]
.
Within the vast literature concerning plane partitions, we highlight Proctor’s work
[
36
]
relating various families of bounded plane partitions to weight bases for representations of classical Lie groups.

1.4.
Method of proof

Via Howe duality (see Section
2
), the unitary highest weight modules
L_{\lambda(\sigma)}
can be viewed as
modules of covariants

M_{\sigma}
(see (
2.4
) below) for the complex classical groups
H(k)
from (
1.4
).
The identification
L_{\lambda(\sigma)}\cong M_{\sigma}
, as graded modules over the ring
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{p}^{+}]
, follows from viewing
L_{\lambda(\sigma)}
as the
U_{\sigma}
-isotypic multiplicity space in the Howe duality decomposition (
2.6
).
In our previous work
[
14
]
*Thm. 3.2, we introduced a combinatorial model for
M_{\sigma}

by organizing Jackson’s
[
25
]
linear basis of standard monomials into a
Stanley decomposition
(see Definition
5.3
).
In particular, we expressed
M_{\sigma}
as a direct sum of Stanley spaces indexed by a set
\mathcal{J}_{k}(\sigma)
consisting of combinatorial objects we call
jellyfish
(see Definition
5.6
).
Very roughly speaking, a jellyfish is an ordered pair
(T,\mathbf{F})
where
T
is a semistandard tableau of shape
\sigma
, and
\mathbf{F}
is a union of
k
nonintersecting lattice paths whose endpoints are compatible (in a technical sense) with the initial column of
T
.
Section
5
in the present paper is a self-contained summary of our results from
[
14
]
.

The two invariants in the opening paragraph, namely the Gelfand–Kirillov dimension
\operatorname{Dim}X
and the Bernstein degree
\operatorname{Deg}X
, have analogues in the setting of graded modules:
these are the
Krull dimension

\operatorname{Kdim}M
and the
multiplicity

e(M)
, respectively.
Thus, in translating between this paper and our previous paper
[
14
]
, we have
\operatorname{Deg}L_{\lambda(\sigma)}=e(M_{\sigma})
.
In turn, it is a general fact (see Lemma
5.4
) that for any Stanley decomposition of a graded module
M
, the multiplicity
e(M)
equals the number of Stanley spaces of maximum Krull dimension.
Thus by identifying the subset
\widehat{\mathcal{J}}_{k}(\sigma)
of jellyfish with maximum Krull dimension, we obtain the following (see Lemma
5.7
):

\operatorname{Deg}L_{\lambda(\sigma)}=e(M_{\sigma})=\#\widehat{\mathcal{J}}_{k}(\sigma).

The heart of the argument, leading immediately to Theorem
1.1
, occurs in Lemma
6.1
, where we establish a bijection

(1.9)

{\rm id}\times\Theta:\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)\times\mathcal{P}_{k}\longrightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{J}}_{k}(\sigma),

where
\Theta
takes a plane partition in
\mathcal{P}_{k}
to its corresponding union of lattice paths.
In this way, upon restriction to the
k\leq r
case, we produce a new and very different proof of the Nishiyama–Ochiai–Taniguchi result (
1.7
), in addition to extending it to arbitrary values of
k
.

We mentioned above that in the case
G_{\mathbb{R}}=\Mp(2n,\mathbb{R})
, Theorem
1.1
remains partially conjectural, namely in the range
r<k<s
.
(See Conjecture
6.3
, which we have verified extensively by computer.)
In the
\U(p,q)
and
\operatorname{O}^{*}(2n)
cases, our construction of the jellyfish in
[
14
]
was guided by the standard monomial results in Jackson
[
25
]
, whereas the
\operatorname{Mp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
case (being considerably more delicate) fell outside the scope of Jackson’s methods.
Hence in the
\operatorname{Mp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
case, our proof of Theorem
1.1
for
k\leq r
and
k\geq s
appeals to (
1.7
)–(
1.8
).

1.5.
Beyond the dual pair setting

The ultimate goal of this research program is to generalize Theorem
1.1
uniformly outside the dual pair setting.
This goal requires finding analogues for the sets
\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)
and
\mathcal{P}_{k}
, for all groups
G_{\mathbb{R}}
of Hermitian type (including the exceptional groups).
In this paper, we take the first step toward this goal by generalizing the set
\mathcal{P}_{k}
as follows.

Recall that in the dual pair setting detailed above (see also Definition
4.1
), we defined
\mathcal{P}_{k}
as the set of plane partitions bounded by
k
and contained in a certain diagram
D_{k}
(either a rectangle or a staircase).
By redefining these underlying diagrams
D_{k}
in terms of the Hasse diagram of the poset
\Phi(\mathfrak{p}^{+})
of positive noncompact roots (see Definition
7.4
), we obtain analogous diagrams
D_{k}
across all Hermitian types.
We refer the reader to Table
4
(page
4
) for the depictions of these
D_{k}
’s in all types.
In this way, we generalize not only the motivating property
\#\mathcal{P}_{k}=\deg\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{k}
, but also the combinatorial interpretation of the Hilbert series of the coordinate ring
\mathbb{C}[\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{k}]
in terms of
\mathcal{P}_{k}
.
In the following proposition (which we prove in Section
7
, restated as Proposition
7.6
), given a plane partition
P\in\mathcal{P}_{k}
, the statistic
c(P)
is obtained by summing local differences over all the entries in
P
; see (
7.3
).
We write
|D_{k}|
to denote the number of boxes in the diagram
D_{k}
.

Proposition 1.2
.

Let
(G_{\mathbb{R}},K_{\mathbb{R}})
be an irreducible Hermitian symmetric pair of noncompact type, and let
r
denote the real rank of
G_{\mathbb{R}}
.
For all
1\leq k\leq r
, we have the Hilbert series

H_{\mathbb{C}[\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{k}]}(t)=\frac{\sum_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{k}}t^{c(P)}}{(1-t)^{\dim\mathfrak{p}^{+}-|D_{k}|}}.

(As mentioned above, in the dual pair setting, Proposition
1.2
can be easily aligned with the equivalent result obtained in the 1990s
[
41
,
6
,
3
,
17
,
4
]
for generic, symmetric, and skew-symmetric determinantal rings, via well-known bijections between plane partitions and families of nonintersecting lattice paths.)
We conclude Section
7
by observing analogues of the Nishiyama–Ochiai–Taniguchi result (
1.7
) where
G_{\mathbb{R}}
is one of the exceptional groups
\operatorname{E}_{6}
or
\operatorname{E}_{7}
;
see Table
3
(page
3
).
These striking examples suggest the existence of a new analogue of Howe duality for the exceptional groups.

2.
Howe duality

2.1.
Hermitian symmetric pairs

Suppose
G_{\mathbb{R}}
is a connected real reductive group, with real rank
r
.
Further suppose that
K_{\mathbb{R}}\subset G_{\mathbb{R}}
is a maximal compact subgroup such that
G_{\mathbb{R}}/K_{\mathbb{R}}
is an irreducible Hermitian symmetric space of noncompact type.
Let
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{R}}=\mathfrak{k}_{\mathbb{R}}\oplus\mathfrak{p}_{\mathbb{R}}
be a
Cartan decomposition of the Lie algebra of
G_{\mathbb{R}}
, and let

\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{k}\oplus\mathfrak{p}
be the corresponding decomposition
of the complexified Lie algebra.
From the general theory of Hermitian symmetric pairs, there exists a distinguished element
h_{0}\in\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{k})
such that
\operatorname{ad}h_{0}
acts on
\mathfrak{g}
with eigenvalues
0
and
\pm 1
.
We thus have a triangular decomposition

\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{p}^{-}\oplus\mathfrak{k}\oplus\mathfrak{p}^{+},

where
\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}=\{x\in\mathfrak{g}:[h_{0},x]=\pm x\}
.
The subalgebra
\mathfrak{q}=\mathfrak{k}\oplus\mathfrak{p}^{+}
is a maximal parabolic subalgebra of
\mathfrak{g}
, with Levi subalgebra
\mathfrak{k}
and abelian nilradical
\mathfrak{p}^{+}
.
Upon fixing a Cartan subalgebra
\mathfrak{h}
of both
\mathfrak{g}
and
\mathfrak{k}
, we write
\Phi
for the root system of
(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h})
, and
\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
for the root space corresponding to the root
\alpha\in\Phi
.
Then we put

(2.1)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Phi(\mathfrak{p}^{+})&\coloneqq\{\alpha\in\Phi:\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}\subseteq\mathfrak{p}^{+}\},\\
S&\coloneqq\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{p}^{+}]=\mathbb{C}[z_{ij}],\end{split}

where the
z_{ij}
’s are the matrix coordinate functions on
\mathfrak{p}^{+}
(see Table
1
).

Let
K
be the complexification of
K_{\mathbb{R}}
.
Then
\mathfrak{k}
is the Lie algebra of
K
, and the adjoint action of
\mathfrak{k}
on

\mathfrak{g}
exponentiates to a
K
-action.
In particular,
K
acts on
\mathfrak{p}^{+}
.
The
K
-orbits in
\mathfrak{p}^{+}
are

\scr{O}_{0}:=\{0\}
and

\mathcal{O}_{k}:=K\cdot(e_{\gamma_{1}}+\dots+e_{\gamma_{k}})
for
1\leq k\leq r
, where
e_{\gamma_{i}}\in\mathfrak{g}_{\gamma_{i}}
denotes a root vector corresponding to
\gamma_{i}
, and where
\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{r}
are Harish-Chandra’s set of strongly orthogonal roots.
Recall from (
1.2
) that the closures of the
K
-orbits in
\mathfrak{p}^{+}
form a chain of algebraic varieties

\{0\}=\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{0}\subset\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\subset\cdots\subset\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{r}=\mathfrak{p}^{+}.

G_{\mathbb{R}}

\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{R}}

\mathfrak{g}

K

\mathfrak{g}=\left\{\left[\begin{smallmatrix}A&B\\
C&D\end{smallmatrix}\right]:\ldots\right\}

\mathfrak{k}=\left\{\left[\begin{smallmatrix}A&0\\
0&D\end{smallmatrix}\right]\right\}

\mathfrak{p}^{+}=\left\{\left[\begin{smallmatrix}0&B\\
0&0\end{smallmatrix}\right]\right\}

[2pt]

\U(p,q)

\mathfrak{u}(p,q)

\mathfrak{gl}_{p+q}

\GL_{p}\times\GL_{q}

no constraints

\mathfrak{gl}_{p}\oplus\mathfrak{gl}_{q}

\operatorname{M}_{p,q}

\operatorname{Mp}(2n,\mathbb{R})

\mathfrak{sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})

\mathfrak{sp}_{2n}

\widetilde{\GL}_{n}

A=-D^{t}
,

B=B^{t}
,

C=C^{t}

\mathfrak{gl}_{n}

\operatorname{SM}_{n}

\operatorname{O}^{*}(2n)

\mathfrak{so}^{*}(2n)

\mathfrak{so}_{2n}

\GL_{n}

A=-D^{t}
,

B=-B^{t}
,

C=-C^{t}

\mathfrak{gl}_{n}

\operatorname{AM}_{n}

Table 1
.
The real reductive groups
G_{\mathbb{R}}
arising in the dual pair setting; see Table
2
for the corresponding complex classical groups
H(k)
.
Note that
\widetilde{\GL}_{n}
denotes the double cover
\widetilde{\GL}_{n}\coloneqq\{(g,s)\in\GL_{n}\times\,\mathbb{C}^{\times}:\det(g)=s^{2}\}
.

2.2.
Howe duality

In this paper, we focus on the three groups in Table
1
, namely
G_{\mathbb{R}}=\U(p,q)
,
\operatorname{Mp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
, or
\operatorname{O}^{*}(2n)
, defined as follows:

\displaystyle\U(p,q)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{g\in\GL(p+q,\mathbb{C}):g\begin{pmatrix}I_{p}&0\\
0&-I_{q}\end{pmatrix}g^{*}=\begin{pmatrix}I_{p}&0\\
0&-I_{q}\end{pmatrix}\right\},

\displaystyle\Sp(2n,\mathbb{R})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{g\in\GL(2n,\mathbb{C}):g\begin{pmatrix}0&I_{n}\\
-I_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}g^{t}=\begin{pmatrix}0&I_{n}\\
-I_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}\right\}\cap\U(n,n),

\displaystyle\operatorname{Mp}(2n,\mathbb{R})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\text{the metaplectic double cover of $\Sp(2n,\mathbb{R})$},

\displaystyle\operatorname{O}^{*}(2n)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{g\in\GL(2n,\mathbb{C}):g\begin{pmatrix}0&I_{n}\\
I_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}g^{t}=\begin{pmatrix}0&I_{n}\\
I_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}\right\}\cap\U(n,n),

where
I_{n}
denotes the
n\times n
identity matrix.
Note that we have

\U(p,q)\cap\U(p+q)\cong\U(p)\times\U(q)
, and
\Sp(2n,\mathbb{R})\cap\U(2n)\cong\U(n)
, and
\operatorname{O}^{*}(2n)\cap\U(2n)\cong\U(n)
,
where in the last two cases
\U(n)

is embedded block-diagonally as follows:

\left\{\begin{pmatrix}a&0\\
0&(a^{-1})^{t}\end{pmatrix}:a\in\U(n)\right\}\cong\U(n).

It follows that

\Sp(2n,\mathbb{R})\subseteq\operatorname{SL}(2n,\mathbb{C})
and
\operatorname{O}^{*}(2n)\subseteq\operatorname{SL}(2n,\mathbb{C})
.
For this reason, many authors write
\operatorname{SO}^{*}(2n)
to denote
\operatorname{O}^{*}(2n)
.
From now on, we denote the
complex
classical groups by
\GL_{n}\coloneqq\GL(n,\mathbb{C})
and
\operatorname{O}_{n}\coloneqq\operatorname{O}(n,\mathbb{C})
, and
\Sp_{2n}\coloneqq\Sp(2n,\mathbb{C})
.

Remark 2.1
.

Our definition of
\Sp(2n,\mathbb{R})
above
differs from the more standard definition
by a Cayley transform as follows.
Let
\mathbf{c}\in\operatorname{SL}(2n,\mathbb{C})
be the matrix given by

\mathbf{c}\coloneqq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}I_{n}&\sqrt{-1}I_{n}\\
\sqrt{-1}I_{n}&I_{n}\end{pmatrix}.

Then
\mathbf{c}\Sp(2n,\mathbb{R})\mathbf{c}^{-1}\subseteq\operatorname{SL}(2n,\mathbb{R})
, with equality if and only if
n=1
.

Let
k
be a positive integer.
Corresponding to each group
G_{\mathbb{R}}
in Table
1
is a compact group
H_{\mathbb{R}}(k)
, giving rise to the following
compact dual pairs

(G_{\mathbb{R}},H_{\mathbb{R}}(k))
:

(\U(p,q),\>\U(k)),\qquad(\operatorname{Mp}(2n,\mathbb{R}),\>\operatorname{O}(k)),\qquad(\operatorname{O}^{*}(2n),\>\Sp(k)).

Let
H(k)
denote the complexification of
H_{\mathbb{R}}(k)
, given in (
2.2
) below.
The group
H(k)
acts naturally on the following space
W
of complex matrices, as follows:

(2.2)

\begin{array}[]{|c|c|c|c|}\hline\cr G_{\mathbb{R}}&H(k)&W&\text{$H(k)$-action on $W$}\\
\hline\cr\U(p,q)&\GL_{k}&\operatorname{M}_{p,k}\oplus\operatorname{M}_{k,q}&h\cdot(X,Y)=(Xh^{-1},\>hY)\\
\hline\cr\operatorname{Mp}(2n,\mathbb{R})&\operatorname{O}_{k}&\operatorname{M}_{k,n}&h\cdot X=hX\\
\hline\cr\operatorname{O}^{*}(2n)&\Sp_{2k}&\operatorname{M}_{2k,n}&h\cdot X=hX\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

This induces the usual action by
H(k)
on
\mathbb{C}[W]
, via
(h\cdot f)(w)=f(h^{-1}\cdot w)
.
Let
\mathcal{D}(W)
denote the Weyl algebra of polynomial-coefficient differential operators on
\mathbb{C}[W]
, and let
\mathcal{D}(W)^{H(k)}
denote the subalgebra of
H(k)
-invariant operators.
Then the complexified Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
of
G_{\mathbb{R}}
embeds in
\mathcal{D}(W)^{H(k)}
as a Lie subalgebra, and is spanned by a generating set of the associative algebra
\mathcal{D}(W)^{H(k)}
.
Let

\omega:\mathfrak{g}\longrightarrow\mathcal{D}(W)^{H(k)}

denote the injective homomorphism;
then
\mathfrak{g}
acts on
\mathbb{C}[W]
via its image
\omega(\mathfrak{g})
in
\mathcal{D}(W)^{H(k)}
.
For details, see the appendix in
[
14
]
, where we write down explicitly the homomorphism
\omega
and the action of
\mathfrak{g}
by differential operators.

Let
\widehat{H}(k)
denote the set of irreducible rational representations of
H(k)
, up to equivalence.
It is standard to view the elements
\sigma\in\widehat{H}(k)
as partitions (or pairs of partitions, for
\GL_{k}
), as given in Table
2
.
We recall that a
partition
is a finite, weakly decreasing sequence of positive integers.
Given a partition
\sigma=(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{m})
, we write
\ell(\sigma)\coloneqq m
for its
length
.
We write
\sigma^{\prime}_{j}\coloneqq\#\{i:\sigma_{i}\geq j\}
.
When
H(k)=\GL_{k}
, we will generalize the notion of a partition by considering decreasing
k
-tuples
\sigma
of (possibly nonpositive) integers.
Any such
k
-tuple can be expressed uniquely as a pair
\sigma=(\sigma^{+},\sigma^{-})
, where
\sigma^{+}
and
\sigma^{-}
are true partitions:
in particular,
\sigma^{+}
consists of the positive coordinates of
\sigma
, and
\sigma^{-}
is the partition obtained by negating and reversing the negative coordinates of
\sigma
.
For example, if
\sigma=(6,3,3,2,0,0,-1,-3,-5)
, then
\sigma^{+}=(6,3,3,2)
and
\sigma^{-}=(5,3,1)
.

Let
U_{\sigma}
denote a model for the irreducible representation
\sigma\in\widehat{H}(k)
.
If
H(k)=\Sp_{2k}
or
\GL_{k}
, then
U_{\sigma}
is the irreducible representation of
H(k)
with highest weight
\sigma
(in standard coordinates).
For
H(k)=\operatorname{O}_{k}
, which is not connected, the situation is more subtle; see
[
19
]
*pp. 438–9 for a detailed construction of the representations
U_{\sigma}
.
If
\sigma\in\widehat{H}(k)
,
then
\mathcal{D}(W)^{H(k)}
and hence
\mathfrak{g}

acts on the multiplicity space

(2.3)

M_{\sigma}\coloneqq{\rm Hom}_{H(k)}(U_{\sigma}^{*},\mathbb{C}[W]).

The action of an operator
D\in\mathcal{D}(W)^{H(k)}
on
\varphi\in M_{\sigma}
is given by

D\cdot\varphi=D\circ\varphi
.
The algebra
\mathcal{D}(W)^{H(k)}
acts irreducibly on
M_{\sigma}
, and hence
\mathfrak{g}
does as well.
Due to the canonical isomorphisms

(2.4)

M_{\sigma}\cong(\mathbb{C}[W]\otimes U_{\sigma})^{H(k)}\cong\Big\{\text{$H(k)$-equivariant polynomial functions from $W$ to $U_{\sigma}$}\Big\},

one can identify
M_{\sigma}
with the
module of covariants
on the right-hand side of (
2.4
); see
[
14
]
*§4.

Roughly speaking, a
(\mathfrak{g},K)
-module
is a
complex vector space carrying representations of both
\mathfrak{g}
and
K
, such that
K
acts locally finitely and the actions of
\mathfrak{g}

and
K
are compatible; see
[
38
]
*Def. 3.2.3 for details.
In the dual pair setting, the “classical”
\mathfrak{k}
-action on
M_{\sigma}
is given by the differential of the right matrix multiplication by
K
on
W
, but this classical action is
not
the action obtained by restricting the
\mathfrak{g}
-action to
\mathfrak{k}
.
Instead, we should view
M_{\sigma}
as a
\mathfrak{k}
-module on which the classical
\mathfrak{k}
-action is “twisted” by multiplying by a suitable power of the determinant.
Hence, writing
M_{\sigma}^{\text{class.}}
to denote the
\mathfrak{k}
-module with the classical action, we will view
M_{\sigma}
as the
\mathfrak{k}
-module
M_{\sigma}^{\text{class.}}\otimes F_{-kc\zeta}
, where
F_{-kc\zeta}
is a certain one-dimensional
\mathfrak{k}
-module;
in particular,
c
is a certain constant intrinsic to
G_{\mathbb{R}}/K_{\mathbb{R}}
(see
[
2
]
*§2), and
\zeta
is the unique fundamental weight of
\mathfrak{g}
that is orthogonal to the compact roots.
In this way, the twisted
\mathfrak{k}
-action on
M_{\sigma}
integrates to a
K
-action in a manner compatible with the
\mathfrak{g}
-action, and hence

M_{\sigma}
can be viewed as a
(\mathfrak{g},K)
-module.
Further,
M_{\sigma}
is a highest weight
\mathfrak{g}
-module, which follows from work of Harish-Chandra
[
20
,
21
]
since
G_{\mathbb{R}}/K_{\mathbb{R}}
is Hermitian symmetric.
We denote this highest weight by
\lambda(\sigma)
.
To write down
\lambda(\sigma)
in coordinates, we introduce the following shorthand for a weight of
\mathfrak{g}
:

(2.5)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\rightarrow\scriptstyle\crcr\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\sigma\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\rightarrow\scriptstyle\crcr\cr$\hfil\textstyle\sigma\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\rightarrow\scriptscriptstyle\crcr\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\sigma\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\rightarrow\scriptscriptstyle\crcr\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\sigma\hfil$\crcr}}}&\coloneqq\text{vector obtained from $\sigma$ by padding with 0's on the right},\\
\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\leftarrow\scriptstyle\crcr\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\sigma\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\leftarrow\scriptstyle\crcr\cr$\hfil\textstyle\sigma\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\leftarrow\scriptscriptstyle\crcr\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\sigma\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\leftarrow\scriptscriptstyle\crcr\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\sigma\hfil$\crcr}}}&\coloneqq\text{vector obtained by reversing $\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\rightarrow\scriptstyle\crcr\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\sigma\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\rightarrow\scriptstyle\crcr\cr$\hfil\textstyle\sigma\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\rightarrow\scriptscriptstyle\crcr\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\sigma\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\rightarrow\scriptscriptstyle\crcr\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\sigma\hfil$\crcr}}}$},\\
\mu-k&\coloneqq\text{vector obtained from $\mu$ by subtracting $k$ from every coordinate}.\end{split}

In Table
2
, we write weights for
\mathfrak{g}
in standard epsilon coordinates on the Cartan subalgebra consisting of diagonal matrices.
In particular, a weight for
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{gl}_{p+q}
is written as
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{p}\mid b_{1},\ldots,b_{q})
;
a weight for
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sp}_{2n}
or
\mathfrak{so}_{2n}
is written as
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})
.

Theorem 2.2

(Howe duality; see
[
24
]
,
[
28
]
)
.

Assume one of the three settings in Tables
1
and
2
, with
W
as in (
2.2
).
We have the following multiplicity-free decomposition as a
(\mathfrak{g},K)\times H(k)
-module:

(2.6)

\mathbb{C}[W]\cong\bigoplus_{\sigma\in\Sigma}M_{\sigma}\otimes U_{\sigma}^{*},

where
M_{\sigma}
is the multiplicity space in (
2.3
), and where
\Sigma\coloneqq\{\sigma\in\widehat{H}(k):M_{\sigma}\neq 0\}
.
Furthermore, the map
\sigma\mapsto\lambda(\sigma)
is an injective map from
\Sigma
into the set of
\mathfrak{k}
-dominant integral weights, such that as a
(\mathfrak{g},K)
-module

(2.7)

M_{\sigma}\cong L_{\lambda(\sigma)}\coloneqq\textup{simple $\mathfrak{g}$-module with highest weight $\lambda(\sigma)$},

and
L_{\lambda(\sigma)}
is unitarizable with respect to
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{R}}
.

G_{\mathbb{R}}

Table 2
.
Companion table to Table
1
; see Theorem
2.2
.
The parameter
r
is the real rank of
G_{\mathbb{R}}
, and
s
is the sharp bound such that
k\geq s
implies that
L_{\lambda(\sigma)}
is free for all
\sigma\in\widehat{H}(k)
.
The arrow notation for
\lambda(\sigma)
is the shorthand given in (
2.5
).

3.
The set
\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)

3.1.
Semistandard tableaux

Recall the definition of a
partition
from Section
2.2
.
We adopt the shorthand
(a^{m})\coloneqq(a,\ldots,a)
, and we write
0
to denote the empty partition, which has length
0
.
The
Young diagram
associated to a partition
\sigma
is an arrangement of boxes in left-justified rows, such that the
i
th row from the top contains
\sigma_{i}
boxes.
Note that
\sigma^{\prime}_{j}\coloneqq\#\{i:\sigma_{i}\leq j\}
can be viewed as the number of boxes in column
j
of the Young diagram of
\sigma
.

A
semistandard Young tableau
is a Young diagram in which the boxes are filled with positive integer entries, weakly increasing across each row and strictly increasing down each column.
We write closed intervals of integers using the standard notation
[m,n]\coloneqq\{x\in\mathbb{Z}:m\leq x\leq n\}
, and we adopt the usual shorthand
[n]\coloneqq[1,n]
.
The
shape
of a tableau is the partition corresponding to its underlying Young diagram.
The empty tableau
\varnothing
is the tableau with shape
0
.
We write

\SSYT(\sigma,n)\coloneqq\Big\{\text{semistandard Young tableaux of shape $\sigma$, with entries taken from $[n]$}\Big\}.

Below, we show an example of a partition
\sigma
along with a tableau
T\in\SSYT(\sigma,n)
:

(3.1)

\sigma=(3,2,1,1)\quad\leadsto\quad\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to8.4pt{\vss\hbox to8.00006pt{\hss$\scriptstyle 1$\hss}\vss}\kern-8.4pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-8.4pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to8.4pt{\vss\hbox to8.00006pt{\hss$\scriptstyle 2$\hss}\vss}\kern-8.4pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-8.4pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to8.4pt{\vss\hbox to8.00006pt{\hss$\scriptstyle 6$\hss}\vss}\kern-8.4pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-8.4pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to8.4pt{\vss\hbox to8.00006pt{\hss$\scriptstyle 3$\hss}\vss}\kern-8.4pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-8.4pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to8.4pt{\vss\hbox to8.00006pt{\hss$\scriptstyle 3$\hss}\vss}\kern-8.4pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-8.4pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to8.4pt{\vss\hbox to8.00006pt{\hss$\scriptstyle 5$\hss}\vss}\kern-8.4pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-8.4pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to8.4pt{\vss\hbox to8.00006pt{\hss$\scriptstyle 6$\hss}\vss}\kern-8.4pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-8.4pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr}}\kern 690.4pt}}$}\in\SSYT(\sigma,n),\text{ where $n\geq 6$.}

Note that if
\ell(\sigma)>n
, then we automatically have
\SSYT(\sigma,n)=\varnothing
.
On the other hand, if
\sigma=0
is the empty partition, then
\SSYT(0,n)=\{\varnothing\}
is the singleton consisting of the empty tableau.
Given a tableau
T
, we write

\displaystyle T_{1}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\text{set of entries in column $1$ of $T$},

\displaystyle T_{1}\uplus T_{2}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\text{multiset of entries in columns $1$ and $2$ of $T$},

where we count columns from the left.
For the tableau
T
displayed in (
3.1
), we have
T_{1}=\{1,3,5,6\}
and
T_{1}\uplus T_{2}=\{\!\!\{1,2,3,3,5,6\}\!\!\}
.
In the
G_{\mathbb{R}}=\U(p,q)
setting, where
\widehat{H}(k)
consists of pairs
\sigma=(\sigma^{+},\sigma^{-})
, a tableau
T
of shape
\sigma
is an ordered pair
T=(T^{+},T^{-})
of tableaux such that
T^{\pm}
has shape
\sigma^{\pm}
.

3.2.
The set
\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)

In Definition
3.1
below, we define
\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)
as a subset of a full set
\mathcal{T}(\sigma)
of semistandard tableaux of shape
\sigma
.
As mentioned above, for
G_{\mathbb{R}}=\U(p,q)
, each “tableau”
T\in\mathcal{T}(\sigma)
is actually an ordered pair
T=(T^{+},T^{-})
.

Definition 3.1
.

Assume one of the three settings in Table
2
.
For
\sigma\in\widehat{H}(k)
, define

\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)\coloneqq\left\{T\in\mathcal{T}(\sigma):\begin{array}[]{l}\alpha_{i}(T)<i,\\
\text{for all positive integers $i$ such that}\\
k-r<i\leq k\end{array}\right\},

where
\mathcal{T}(\sigma)
and
\alpha_{i}(T)
are defined as follows:

G_{\mathbb{R}}

\mathcal{T}(\sigma)

\alpha_{i}(T)

[2pt]

\U(p,q)

\phantom{\times}\SSYT(\sigma^{+},q)

\times\SSYT(\sigma^{-},p)

\phantom{+}\;\#\{x\in T^{+}_{1}:x<q-k+i\}

+\;\#\{y\in T^{-}_{1}:y<p-k+i\}

\operatorname{Mp}(2n,\mathbb{R})

\SSYT(\sigma,n)

\#\{x\in T_{1}\uplus T_{2}:x<n-k+i\}

\operatorname{O}^{*}(2n)

\SSYT(\sigma,n)

\#\{x\in T_{1}:x<n-1-2k+2i\}

Comparing the
\Sigma
column in Table
2
with Definition
3.1
, we observe that
\sigma\in\Sigma
if and only if
\mathcal{T}(\sigma)\neq\varnothing
.

Remark 3.2
.

For
G_{\mathbb{R}}=\operatorname{Mp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
or
\operatorname{O}^{*}(2n)
, it is straightforward to verify by Definition
3.1
that the constraint
\alpha_{i}(T)<i
is trivial if
i\leq k-r
;
hence there is no harm in omitting the lower bound on
i
, so that
1\leq i\leq k
.
By contrast, for
G_{\mathbb{R}}=\U(p,q)
, the constraint
\alpha_{i}(T)<i
is not necessarily trivial for
i\leq k-r
, so we cannot omit this lower bound without changing the definition of
\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)
.
On the other hand, if one wishes to
avoid
checking trivial constraints (see Example
3.3
below), then one need only check positive integers
i
such that

\begin{cases}\min\{2k-s,\>k-r\}<i\leq\ell(\sigma^{+})+\ell(\sigma^{-})&\text{if }G_{\mathbb{R}}=\U(p,q),\\
2k-s<i\leq\sigma^{\prime}_{1}+\sigma^{\prime}_{2}&\text{if }G_{\mathbb{R}}=\operatorname{Mp}(2n,\mathbb{R}),\\
2k-s<i\leq\ell(\sigma)&\text{if }G_{\mathbb{R}}=\operatorname{O}^{*}(2n).\end{cases}

Example 3.3
.

(1)

Let
G_{\mathbb{R}}=\U(p,q)
, where
(p,q)=(3,5)
and
k=5
.
By Table
2
, we have
r=3
, so
k-r=2
.
In
\alpha_{i}(T)
in Definition
3.1
, the conditions are
x<q-k+i=i
and
y<p-k+i=i-2
.
Thus, the set
\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)
consists of those tableaux
T\in\mathcal{T}(\sigma)
such that
\#\{x\in T^{+}_{1}:x<i\}+\#\{y\in T^{-}_{1}:y<i-2\}<i
, for
i=3,4,5
:

\displaystyle\#\{x\in T^{+}_{1}:x<3\}+\#\{y\in T^{-}_{1}:y<1\}

\displaystyle<3,

\displaystyle\#\{x\in T^{+}_{1}:x<4\}+\#\{y\in T^{-}_{1}:y<2\}

\displaystyle<4,

\displaystyle\#\{x\in T^{+}_{1}:x<5\}+\#\{y\in T^{-}_{1}:y<3\}

\displaystyle<5.

(Note that the first constraint is trivial, since it is satisfied by any semistandard tableau.)
Thus we have

\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)=\left\{T\in\mathcal{T}(\sigma):(T^{+},T^{-})\text{ has initial columns }\left(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\scriptstyle\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower (2)

Let
G_{\mathbb{R}}=\operatorname{Mp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
, where
n=4
and
k=5
.
By Table
2
, we have
r=4
, so
k-r=1
.
In
\alpha_{i}(T)
in Definition
3.1
, the condition is
x<n-k+i=i-1
.
Thus,
\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)
consists of those tableaux
T\in\mathcal{T}(\sigma)
such that
\#\{x\in T_{1}\uplus T_{2}:x<i-1\}<i
, for
i=2,3,4,5
:

\displaystyle\#\{x\in T_{1}\uplus T_{2}:x<1\}

\displaystyle<2,

\displaystyle\#\{x\in T_{1}\uplus T_{2}:x<2\}

\displaystyle<3,

\displaystyle\#\{x\in T_{1}\uplus T_{2}:x<3\}

\displaystyle<4,

\displaystyle\#\{x\in T_{1}\uplus T_{2}:x<4\}

\displaystyle<5.

(Note that the first two constraints are trivial.)
Thus we have

(3)

Let
G_{\mathbb{R}}=\operatorname{O}^{*}(2n)
, where
n=7
and
k=4
.
By Table
2
, we have
r=3
, so
k-r=1
.
In
\alpha_{i}(T)
in Definition
3.1
, the condition on
x\in T_{1}
is
x<n-1-2k+2i=2i-2
.
Thus, the set
\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)
consists of those tableaux
T\in\mathcal{T}(\sigma)
such that
\#\{x\in T_{1}:x<2i-2\}<i
, for
i=2,3,4
:

\displaystyle\#\{x\in T_{1}:x<2\}

\displaystyle<2,

\displaystyle\#\{x\in T_{1}:x<4\}

\displaystyle<3,

\displaystyle\#\{x\in T_{1}:x<6\}

\displaystyle<4.

(Note that the first constraint is trivial.)
Thus we have

\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)=\left\{T\in\mathcal{T}(\sigma):T\text{ has initial column \, $\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\scriptstyle\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to12.39992pt{\vss\hbox to11.99998pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\ast$\hss}\vss}\kern-12.39992pt\vrule height=12.39992pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 11.99998pt\vrule height=12.39992pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-12.39992pt\hrule width=12.79985pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 11.99998pt\hrule width=12.79985pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to12.39992pt{\vss\hbox to11.99998pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\ast$\hss}\vss}\kern-12.39992pt\vrule height=12.39992pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 11.99998pt\vrule height=12.39992pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-12.39992pt\hrule width=12.79985pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 11.99998pt\hrule width=12.79985pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to12.39992pt{\vss\hbox to11.99998pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\geq 4$\hss}\vss}\kern-12.39992pt\vrule height=12.39992pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 11.99998pt\vrule height=12.39992pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-12.39992pt\hrule width=12.79985pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 11.99998pt\hrule width=12.79985pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to12.39992pt{\vss\hbox to11.99998pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\geq 6$\hss}\vss}\kern-12.39992pt\vrule height=12.39992pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 11.99998pt\vrule height=12.39992pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-12.39992pt\hrule width=12.79985pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 11.99998pt\hrule width=12.79985pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr}}\kern 345.4pt}}$}$}\,\right\},

where we keep only the topmost
\ell(\sigma)
many boxes.

Lemma 3.4
.

Assume one of the three settings in Tables
1
and
2
, where
\sigma\in\widehat{H}(k)
.
Let
F_{\lambda(\sigma)}
denote the simple
\mathfrak{k}
-module with highest weight
\lambda(\sigma)
.

(a)

If
k\leq r
, then
\#\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)=\dim U_{\sigma}
.

(b)

If
k\geq s
, then
\#\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)=\dim F_{\lambda(\sigma)}
.

Proof.

(a) Suppose
k\leq r
.
Then by Definition
3.1
, we have
T\in\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)
if and only if
\alpha_{i}(T)<i
for all
1\leq i\leq k
.
Equivalently, in the definition of
\alpha_{i}(T)
and in the condition
\alpha_{i}(T)<i
, one can replace all the strict inequalities
<
by weak inequalities
\leq
, and shift the range of
i
-values so that
0\leq i\leq k-1
.
But at
i=0
, the resulting constraint is trivial, so it may be omitted;
at
i=k
, the resulting constraint is automatically satisfied by the fact that
\sigma\in\widehat{H}(k)
, so there is no harm in including it.
Hence we may still use the range
1\leq i\leq k
.

•

Let
G_{\mathbb{R}}=\U(p,q)
.
Then
U_{\sigma}
is the irreducible representation of
H(k)=\GL_{k}
with highest weight
\sigma
.
Let
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}(\sigma)
denote the set of all pairs
(U^{+},U^{-})\in\SSYT(\sigma^{+},k)\times\SSYT(\sigma^{-},k)
such that
\#\{x\in U^{+}_{1}:x\leq i\}+\#\{y\in U^{-}_{1}:y\leq i\}\leq i
, for all
1\leq i\leq k
.
Then
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}(\sigma)
is Stembridge’s set of
rational tableaux
in
[
40
]
*Def. 2.2.
By
[
40
]
*Prop. 2.4(a), we have
\dim U_{\sigma}=\#\overline{\mathcal{Q}}(\sigma)
.
To complete the proof, we exhibit the bijection
\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)\longrightarrow\overline{\mathcal{Q}}(\sigma)
given by
(T^{+},T^{-})\mapsto(\overline{T}^{+},\overline{T}^{-})
, where
\overline{T}^{+}
(resp.,
\overline{T}^{-}
) is obtained by subtracting
q-k
(resp.,
p-k
) from every entry in
T^{+}
(resp.,
T^{-}
).

•

Let
G_{\mathbb{R}}=\operatorname{Mp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
.
Then
U_{\sigma}
is the irreducible representation of
H(k)=\operatorname{O}_{k}
labeled by
\sigma
.
Let
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}(\sigma)
denote the set of all
U\in\SSYT(\sigma,k)
such that
\#\{x\in U_{1}\uplus U_{2}:x\leq i\}\leq i
, for all
1\leq i\leq k
.
Then
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}(\sigma)
is Proctor’s set of
orthogonal tableaux
in
[
35
]
*p. 30.
By
[
37
]
*Thm. 3.1, we have
\dim U_{\sigma}=\#\overline{\mathcal{Q}}(\sigma)
.
To complete the proof, we exhibit the bijection
\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)\longrightarrow\overline{\mathcal{Q}}(\sigma)
given by
T\mapsto\overline{T}
, where
\overline{T}
is obtained by subtracting
n-k
from every entry in
T
.

•

Let
G_{\mathbb{R}}=\operatorname{O}^{*}(2n)
.
Then
U_{\sigma}
is the irreducible representation of
H(k)=\Sp_{2k}
with highest weight
\sigma
.
Let
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}(\sigma)
denote the set of all
U\in\SSYT(\sigma,2k)
such that
\#\{x\in U_{1}:x\leq 2i\}\leq i
, for all
1\leq i\leq k
.
Then
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}(\sigma)
is Proctor’s set of
symplectic tableaux
in
[
35
]
*p. 30.
By
[
37
]
*Thm. 3.1, we have
\dim U_{\sigma}=\#\overline{\mathcal{Q}}(\sigma)
.
To complete the proof, we exhibit the bijection
\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)\longrightarrow\overline{\mathcal{Q}}(\sigma)
given by
T\mapsto\overline{T}
, where
\overline{T}
is obtained by subtracting
n-2k
from every entry in
T
.

(b) Suppose
k\geq s
.
For each group
G_{\mathbb{R}}
, it is straightforward to check directly from Definition
3.1
that the conditions
\alpha_{i}(T)<i
are all trivial for
k-r<i\leq k
.
Hence we have

(3.2)

\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)=\mathcal{T}(\sigma).

It remains to show that
\#\mathcal{T}(\sigma)=\dim F_{\lambda(\sigma)}
.
If
G_{\mathbb{R}}=\U(p,q)
, then by Table
1
we have
\mathfrak{k}=\mathfrak{gl}_{p}\oplus\mathfrak{gl}_{q}
.
Letting
F_{p}(\mu)
denote the simple
\mathfrak{gl}_{p}
-module with highest weight
\mu
(and similarly for
\mathfrak{gl}_{q}
), we have

\displaystyle\#\mathcal{T}(\sigma)=\#\SSYT(\sigma^{-},p)\cdot\#\SSYT(\sigma^{+},q))

\displaystyle=\dim F_{p}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\rightarrow\scriptstyle\crcr\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\sigma^{-}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\rightarrow\scriptstyle\crcr\cr$\hfil\textstyle\sigma^{-}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\rightarrow\scriptscriptstyle\crcr\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\sigma^{-}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\rightarrow\scriptscriptstyle\crcr\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\sigma^{-}\hfil$\crcr}}})\cdot\dim F_{q}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\rightarrow\scriptstyle\crcr\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\sigma^{+}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\rightarrow\scriptstyle\crcr\cr$\hfil\textstyle\sigma^{+}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\rightarrow\scriptscriptstyle\crcr\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\sigma^{+}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\rightarrow\scriptscriptstyle\crcr\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\sigma^{+}\hfil$\crcr}}})

\displaystyle=\dim\left(F_{p}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\rightarrow\scriptstyle\crcr\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\sigma^{-}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\rightarrow\scriptstyle\crcr\cr$\hfil\textstyle\sigma^{-}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\rightarrow\scriptscriptstyle\crcr\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\sigma^{-}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\rightarrow\scriptscriptstyle\crcr\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\sigma^{-}\hfil$\crcr}}})\otimes F_{p}(k^{p})\right)^{*}\cdot\dim F_{q}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\rightarrow\scriptstyle\crcr\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\sigma^{+}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\rightarrow\scriptstyle\crcr\cr$\hfil\textstyle\sigma^{+}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\rightarrow\scriptscriptstyle\crcr\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\sigma^{+}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\rightarrow\scriptscriptstyle\crcr\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\sigma^{+}\hfil$\crcr}}})

\displaystyle=\dim\left(F_{p}(-\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\leftarrow\scriptstyle\crcr\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\sigma^{-}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\leftarrow\scriptstyle\crcr\cr$\hfil\textstyle\sigma^{-}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\leftarrow\scriptscriptstyle\crcr\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\sigma^{-}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\leftarrow\scriptscriptstyle\crcr\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\sigma^{-}\hfil$\crcr}}}-k)\otimes F_{q}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\rightarrow\scriptstyle\crcr\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\sigma^{+}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\rightarrow\scriptstyle\crcr\cr$\hfil\textstyle\sigma^{+}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\rightarrow\scriptscriptstyle\crcr\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\sigma^{+}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\rightarrow\scriptscriptstyle\crcr\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\sigma^{+}\hfil$\crcr}}})\right)

\displaystyle=\dim F_{(-\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\leftarrow\scriptstyle\crcr\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\sigma^{-}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\leftarrow\scriptstyle\crcr\cr$\hfil\textstyle\sigma^{-}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\leftarrow\scriptscriptstyle\crcr\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\sigma^{-}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\leftarrow\scriptscriptstyle\crcr\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\sigma^{-}\hfil$\crcr}}}-k\;\mid\;\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\rightarrow\scriptstyle\crcr\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\sigma^{+}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\rightarrow\scriptstyle\crcr\cr$\hfil\textstyle\sigma^{+}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\rightarrow\scriptscriptstyle\crcr\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\sigma^{+}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\rightarrow\scriptscriptstyle\crcr\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\sigma^{+}\hfil$\crcr}}})}=\dim F_{\lambda(\sigma)}.

If
G_{\mathbb{R}}=\operatorname{Mp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
or
\operatorname{O}^{*}(2n)
, then by Table
1
we have
\mathfrak{k}=\mathfrak{gl}_{n}
, and since
\mathcal{T}(\sigma)=\SSYT(\sigma,n)
, the proof is just a simpler version of the
\U(p,q)
case above.
∎

The following lemma gives an equivalent (case-by-case) criterion for
T\in\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)
which will be useful in proving Proposition
3.6
.
We write
T_{j,\ell}
to denote the entry in row
j
and column
\ell
of
T
.
For a set
S
of integers, we write
S_{(j)}
to denote the
j
th smallest element of
S
.
Hence we have equivalent notation
T_{j,\ell}=(T_{\ell})_{(j)}
.

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
\sigma\in\widehat{H}(k)
in one of the settings in Table
2
.
Let
T\in\mathcal{T}(\sigma)
as in Definition
3.1
.

(1)

Let
G_{\mathbb{R}}=\U(p,q)
.
Let
\overline{T}^{+}
(resp.,
\overline{T}^{-}
) be the tableau obtained by adding
k-q
(resp.,
k-p
) to each entry of
T^{+}
(resp.,
T^{-}
).
Define the sign

\epsilon\coloneqq\begin{cases}+&\text{if }p\leq q,\\
-&\text{if }p>q.\end{cases}

We have
T\in\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)
if and only if

\overline{T}^{\,-\epsilon}_{1,1}\geq 1,\text{ and }\overline{T}^{\,\epsilon}_{j,1}\geq([k]\setminus\overline{T}^{\,-\epsilon}_{1})_{(j)}

for all positive integers
j
such that
k-\min\{p,q\}<j\leq\ell(\sigma^{\epsilon})
.

(2)

Let
G_{\mathbb{R}}=\operatorname{Mp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
.
Define the interval
I\coloneqq[n-k+1,\>n]
.

We have
T\in\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)
if and only if

T_{1,1}\geq n-k+1,\text{ and }T_{j,2}\geq(I\setminus T_{1})_{(j)}

for all positive integers
j
such that
1\leq j\leq\sigma^{\prime}_{2}
.

(3)

Let
G_{\mathbb{R}}=\operatorname{O}^{*}(2n)
.
We have
T\in\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)
if and only if

T_{j,1}\geq n+2(j-k)-1\quad\text{for all }1\leq j\leq\ell(\sigma).

Proof.

(1) Let
G_{\mathbb{R}}=\U(p,q)
.
Suppose
p\leq q
; then we have
r=p
, and
T^{\,\epsilon}=T^{+}
and
T^{-\epsilon}=T^{-}
.
By Definition
3.1
, we have
T\in\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)
if and only if
\#\{x\in\overline{T}^{+}_{1}:x<i\}+\#\{y\in\overline{T}^{-}_{1}:y<i\}<i
for all
\max\{0,k-p\}<i\leq k
.
Equivalently, shifting the index
i
by 1, we have
T\in\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)
if and only if

(3.3)

\#\{x\in\overline{T}^{+}_{1}:x\leq i\}+\#\{y\in\overline{T}^{-}_{1}:y\leq i\}\leq i,\quad\text{for all }\max\{0,\>k-p\}\leq i\leq k-1.

If
\overline{T}^{-}_{1,1}\leq 0
, then
k<p
, and so
i=0
lies in the interval
\max\{0,k-p\}\leq i\leq k
;
but then (
3.3
) is violated for
i=0
, and thus
T\notin\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)
.
Therefore,
T\in\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)
implies
\overline{T}^{-}_{1,1}\geq 1
, as claimed in this lemma.
Consequently, we have
\overline{T}^{-}_{1}\subseteq[k]
, and so (
3.3
) is equivalent to the following:

(3.4)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\#\{x\in\overline{T}^{+}_{1}:x\leq i\}&\leq i-\#\{y\in\overline{T}^{-}_{1}:y\leq i\}\\
&=\#\{z\in([k]\setminus\overline{T}^{-}_{1}):z\leq i\},\quad\text{for all $\max\{0,\>k-p\}\leq i\leq k-1$.}\end{split}

If
k\leq p
, then (
3.4
) occurs if and only if

(3.5)

\overline{T}^{+}_{j,1}\geq([k]\setminus\overline{T}^{-}_{1})_{(j)}

for all
1\leq j\leq\#\overline{T}^{+}_{1}=\ell(\sigma^{+})
, and we are done.
If
k>p
, then
\overline{T}^{-}_{1}\subseteq[k-p+1,\>k]
, and thus
[k]\setminus\overline{T}^{-}_{1}
contains the initial interval
[k-p]
;
in this case, (
3.4
) occurs if and only if (
3.5
) holds for all
k-p<j\leq\ell(\sigma^{+})
, so we are done.
This completes the proof for the
p\leq q
case;
the
p>q
case is identical, upon interchanging the roles of
p
and
q
, and the roles of
\overline{T}^{+}
and
\overline{T}^{-}
.

(2) Let
G_{\mathbb{R}}=\operatorname{Mp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
.
By Definition
3.1
and by Remark
3.2
, we have
T\in\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)
if and only if
\#\{x\in T_{1}\uplus T_{2}:x<n-k+i\}<i
for all
1\leq i\leq k
.
Equivalently,
T\in\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)
if and only if

(3.6)

\#\{x\in T_{1}:x\leq n-k+i\}+\#\{y\in T_{2}:y\leq n-k+i\}\leq i,\quad\text{for all }0\leq i\leq k-1.

If
T_{1,1}\leq n-k
, then
T
violates the
i=0
case in (
3.6
), and we have
T\notin\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)
; hence
T\in\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)
implies
T_{1,1}\geq n-k+1
, as claimed in this lemma.
Thus we may assume that both
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
are subsets of
I=[n-k+1,\>n]
, and so (
3.6
) is equivalent to the following:

\displaystyle\#\{y\in T_{2}:y\leq n-k+i\}

\displaystyle\leq i-\#\{x\in T_{1}:x\leq n-k+i\}

\displaystyle=\#\{z\in(I\setminus T_{1}):z\leq n-k+i\},\quad\text{for all $1\leq i\leq k-1$,}

which occurs if and only if
T_{j,2}\geq(I\setminus T_{1})_{(j)}
for all
1\leq j\leq\#T_{2}=\sigma^{\prime}_{2}
.

(3) Let
G_{\mathbb{R}}=\operatorname{O}^{*}(2n)
.
In Definition
3.1
, replacing
i
by
j
, we have
\alpha_{j}(T)<j
if and only if
\#\{x\in T_{1}:x<n-1-2k+2j\}<j
, if and only if

(3.7)

T_{j,1}\geq n-1-2k+2j=n+2(j-k)-1.

Therefore, by Definition
3.1
, we have
T\in\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)
if and only if (
3.7
) is true for all positive integers
j
such that
k-r<j\leq\#T_{1}=\ell(\sigma)
.
Equivalently, by Remark
3.2
, we can take
1\leq j\leq\ell(\sigma)
.
∎

In the following proposition, we adopt the convention that
\binom{a}{b}=0
if
a
or
b
is negative.

Proposition 3.6
.

Assume one of the three settings in Table
2
, where
\sigma\in\widehat{H}(k)
.
Recall the set
\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)
from Definition
3.1
.

(1)

Let
G_{\mathbb{R}}=\U(p,q)
.
We have

\#\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)=\det\left[\binom{\sigma_{i}-i+j+c_{j}+d_{j}}{\sigma_{i}-i+j+c_{j}}\right]_{1\leq i,j\leq k},

where

c_{j}\coloneqq\begin{cases}0&\textup{if }j\leq k-r,\\
\sigma_{1}^{-\epsilon}&\textup{if }j>k-r\end{cases}\quad\textup{and}\quad d_{j}\coloneqq-1+\begin{cases}R&\textup{if }j\leq k-r,\\
\min\{k,r\}&\textup{if }j>k-r,\end{cases}\vskip 4.30554pt

with
r\coloneqq\min\{p,q\}
and
R\coloneqq\max\{p,q\}
and the sign
\epsilon
as in Lemma
3.5
(1).

(2)

Let
G_{\mathbb{R}}=\operatorname{Mp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
.
We have

\#\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)=\sum_{C}\det\left[\binom{\sigma_{i}-i+j+n-a_{j}(C)-1}{\sigma_{i}-i+j-1}\right]_{1\leq i,j\leq\sigma^{\prime}_{2}}

with the sum ranging over all
\sigma^{\prime}_{1}
-element subsets
C\subseteq[\max\{1,\>n-k+1\},\>n]
, and

a_{j}(C)\coloneqq\max\{C_{(j)},(I\setminus C)_{(j)}\},

where
I=[n-k+1,n]
.

(3)

Let
G_{\mathbb{R}}=\operatorname{O}^{*}(2n)
.
We have

\#\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)=\det\left[\binom{\sigma_{i}-i+j+n-a_{j}}{\sigma_{i}-i+j}\right]_{1\leq i,j\leq k},

where
a_{j}:=\max\{1,\>n+2(j-k)-1\}
.

Proof.

We appeal to a well-known result of Gessel–Viennot
[
16
]
*§6; see also the exposition by Krattenthaler
[
31
]
*§10.13.
In particular, let
\lambda
and
\mu
be partitions of length at most
k
, such that
\mu_{i}\leq\lambda_{i}
for all
1\leq i\leq k
.
Let
\lambda/\mu
denote the skew shape obtained by removing the Young diagram of
\mu
from that of
\lambda
, where the upper-left corners of the diagrams coincide.
Let
\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k})
and
\mathbf{b}=(b_{1},\ldots,b_{k})
be weakly increasing sequences of integers such that each
a_{i}\leq b_{i}
.
Define

\SSYT(\lambda/\mu,\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})\coloneqq\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\text{semistandard Young tableaux of shape $\lambda/\mu$,}\\
\text{where all entries in row $i$ lie in $[a_{i},b_{i}]$}\end{array}\right\}.

Note that
\SSYT(\lambda,n)
is the special case where
\mu=0
and
\mathbf{a}=(1,\ldots,1)
and
\mathbf{b}=(n,\ldots,n)
.
By
[
31
]
*Thm. 10.13.3, we have

(3.8)

\#\SSYT(\lambda/\mu,\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})=\det\left[\binom{\lambda_{i}-\mu_{j}-i+j+b_{i}-a_{j}}{\lambda_{i}-\mu_{j}-i+j}\right]_{1\leq i,j\leq k}.

(1) Let
G_{\mathbb{R}}=\U(p,q)
, and suppose
p\leq q
.
Thus
r=p
,
R=q
, and
\sigma^{-\epsilon}=\sigma^{-}
.
(Just as in Lemma
3.5
(1), the
p>q
case is obtained by switching the roles of
p
and
q
, and of
T^{+}
and
T^{-}
.)
Let
T\in\mathcal{T}(\sigma)
, and define
\overline{T}^{\pm}
as in Lemma
3.5
(1).
We construct a single (possibly skew) semistandard tableau
\overline{T}^{\prime}
as follows:

•

Start with
\overline{T}^{+}
, observing that

(3.9)

\overline{T}^{+}\in\SSYT(\sigma^{+}/0,\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}),\quad\text{where each }a_{i}=k-q+1\text{ and }b_{i}=k.

•

Adjoin the top-justified columns
C_{\sigma^{-}_{1}},\ldots,C_{3},C_{2},C_{1}
to the left of
\overline{T}^{+}
, where each

C_{\ell}\coloneqq[k]\setminus\overline{T}^{-}_{\ell}.

•

If
k>p
, then remove the topmost
k-p
rows from each column
C_{\ell}
above.

Note that the columns
C_{\sigma^{-}},\ldots,C_{1}
form a semistandard tableau
C
with entries in
[k]
; this follows from the same arguments used in the proof of Lemma
3.5
(1), or from a result of Stembridge
[
40
]
*Lemma 2.5(b).
Moreover, since (by Lemma
3.5
)
\overline{T}^{-}
consists of entries in the interval
[\max\{1,\>k-p+1\},k]
, if
j\leq k-p
then the
j
th entry in every column
C_{\ell}
equals
j
;
this is the reason for having removed the rectangular block of
k-p
rows and
\sigma^{-}_{1}
columns from the upper-left (since these entries are all forced, and have no effect on enumerating tableaux).
On the other hand, if
j>k-p
, then by Lemma
3.5
(1), the entry in row
j
of
\overline{T}^{+}_{1}
is no less than the entry in row
j
of
C_{1}
, and therefore the entire tableau
\overline{T}^{\prime}
is semistandard.
By construction,
\overline{T}^{\prime}
has shape
\lambda/\mu
, where
\lambda=\sigma+((\sigma^{-}_{1})^{k})
, and where
\mu=((\sigma^{-}_{1})^{k-p})
if
k>p
and
\mu=0
otherwise.
Note that if
j\leq k-p
, then row
j
in
\overline{T}^{\prime}
is the same as row
j
in
\overline{T}^{+}
, whose entries are bounded below by
a_{j}=k-q+1
and above by
b_{j}=k
, by (
3.9
).
If
j>k-p
, then the initial entries in row
j
come from the
C_{\ell}
’s, whose entries are bounded below by
a_{j}=\max\{1,\>k-p+1\}
and above by
b_{j}=k
.
Hence the map
T\mapsto\overline{T}^{\prime}
gives a bijection

\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)\longrightarrow\SSYT(\lambda/\mu,\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}),

where, for all
1\leq i,j\leq k
,

\lambda_{i}=\sigma_{i}+\sigma^{-}_{1},\qquad\mu_{j}=\begin{cases}\sigma^{-}_{1}&\text{if }j\leq k-p,\\
0&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}\qquad a_{j}=\begin{cases}k-q+1&\text{if }j\leq k-p,\\
\max\{1,\>k-p+1\}&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

and
b_{i}=k
.
The result follows from (
3.8
), upon setting
c_{j}\coloneqq\sigma^{-}_{1}-\mu_{j}
and
d_{j}\coloneqq k-a_{j}
.

(2) Let
G_{\mathbb{R}}=\operatorname{Mp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
.
Given
T\in\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)
, we decompose
T
into its initial column
T_{1}
and the tableau
T_{>1}
consisting of its remaining columns
T_{2},\ldots,T_{\sigma_{1}}
.
By Lemma
3.5
(2), this map
T\mapsto(T_{1},T_{>1})
gives a bijection

\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)\longrightarrow\coprod_{T_{1}}\left(\{T_{1}\}\times\left\{T_{>1}\in\SSYT(\sigma-(1^{\sigma^{\prime}_{1}}),\>n):\begin{array}[]{l}(T_{>1})_{j,1}\geq\max\{(T_{1})_{(j)},(I\setminus T_{1})_{(j)}\\[2.15277pt]
\text{for all }1\leq j\leq\sigma^{\prime}_{2}\end{array}\right\}\right),

where the disjoint union ranges over all possible initial columns
T_{1}
, that is, where
T_{1}
is a
\sigma^{\prime}_{1}
-element subset of
[\max\{1,\>n-k+1\},n]
.
Upon replacing
T_{1}
by
C
, we see that the cardinality of each disjoint component in the right-hand side equals
\#\SSYT(\sigma-(1^{\sigma^{\prime}_{1}}),\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})
, where
a_{j}=\max\{C_{(j)},(I\setminus C)_{(j)}\}
and where every
b_{i}=n
.
The result now follows from (
3.8
).

(3) Let
G_{\mathbb{R}}=\operatorname{O}^{*}(2n)
.
By Lemma
3.5
(3), we have
\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)=\SSYT(\sigma/0,\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})
, where
\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k})
is given by
a_{j}=\max\{1,\>n+2(j-k)-1\}
, and where
\mathbf{b}=(n,\ldots,n)
.
The result follows from (
3.8
).
∎

Remark 3.7
.

In light of Lemma
3.4
, we conclude that the determinantal formulas in Proposition
3.6
must collapse to a simple product formula when
k\leq r
or
k\geq s
.
In particular, if
k\leq r
, then
\#\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)=\dim U_{\sigma}
is given by the well-known hook-content formulas for
H(k)=\GL_{k}
,
\operatorname{O}_{k}
, and
\Sp_{2k}
.
Likewise, if
k\geq s
, then
\#\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)=\dim F_{\lambda(\sigma)}
is given by the hook-content formula for
K=\GL_{p}\times\GL_{q}
or
\GL_{n}
.

4.
The set
\mathcal{P}_{k}

4.1.
Plane partitions and the set
\mathcal{P}_{k}

We use the term
diagram
to describe any edge-connected union
D
of unit squares (
boxes
), not necessarily the Young diagram of a partition.
In particular, the following diagrams play an important role.
A
p\times q
rectangle
is the Young diagram with shape
(q^{p})
.
Following Proctor
[
36
]
, we define an
n
-staircase
to be the Young diagram with shape
(n,\ldots,3,2,1)
, and a
shifted
n
-staircase
to be the horizontal reflection of an
n
-staircase.

Let
k
be a positive integer.
In this paper, a
plane partition
bounded by
k
, and contained in a diagram
D
, is a filling of
D
with entries taken from
[0,k]
, such that the entries weakly increase from left to right, and from bottom to top.

NB:
This means that our two-dimensional pictures of plane partitions are 90-degree clockwise rotations of the usual convention in the literature.
Nonetheless, when using the typical
three
-dimensional depiction of a plane partition — where an entry
t
is represented by a stack of
t
unit cubes — this difference in convention vanishes.
See Figure
1
for an example of the plane partitions in the following definition.

Definition 4.1
.

Assume one of the three settings in Table
2
, and define the diagram

D_{k}\coloneqq\begin{cases}\text{a $(p-k)\times(q-k)$ rectangle}&\text{if $G_{\mathbb{R}}=\U(p,q)$},\\
\text{an $(n-k)$-staircase}&\text{if $G_{\mathbb{R}}=\operatorname{Mp}(2n,\mathbb{R})$},\\
\text{a shifted $(n-2k-1)$-staircase}&\text{if $G_{\mathbb{R}}=\operatorname{O}^{*}(2n)$}.\end{cases}

We define the set

\mathcal{P}_{k}\coloneqq\{\text{plane partitions bounded by $k$ and contained in the diagram $D_{k}$}\}.

See the top row of Figure
1
for examples of the plane partitions in
\mathcal{P}_{k}
.
We observe from Definition
4.1
that if
k\geq r
then
D_{k}=\varnothing
, and thus
\mathcal{P}_{k}=\{\varnothing\}
, where
\varnothing
is the empty plane partition.
Thus,

(4.1)

\text{if $k\geq r$ then $\#\mathcal{P}_{k}=1$}.

Proposition 4.2
.

Let
\mathcal{P}_{k}
be as defined in Definition
4.1
.

(1)

If
G_{\mathbb{R}}=\U(p,q)
, then

\#\mathcal{P}_{k}=\prod_{i=1}^{p-k}\;\;\prod_{j=1}^{q-k}\;\;\frac{k+i+j-1}{i+j-1}.

(2)

If
G_{\mathbb{R}}=\operatorname{Mp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
, then

\#\mathcal{P}_{k}=\prod_{1\leq i\leq j\leq n-k}\frac{k+i+j-1}{i+j-1}.

(3)

If
G_{\mathbb{R}}=\operatorname{O}^{*}(2n)
, then

\#\mathcal{P}_{k}=\prod_{1\leq i\leq j\leq n-2k-1}\frac{2k+i+j}{i+j}.

Proof.

Part (1) is the formula for boxed
(p-k)\times(q-k)\times k
plane partitions due to MacMahon
[
32
]
*§IX.
Part (2) is the formula for symmetric boxed
(n-k)\times(n-k)\times k
plane partitions, conjectured by MacMahon and ultimately proved by Andrews
[
1
]
*§2.
Part (3) is given by Proctor
[
36
]
*Thm. 1, upon setting
q=1
in his formula (CGH) or (CGI).
∎

4.2.
Plane partitions, lattice paths, and order complexes

We next highlight a well-known bijection between plane partitions and families of nonintersecting lattice paths.
To make this precise, we introduce a poset
(\mathbf{P},\leq)
whose underlying set
\mathbf{P}\subset\mathbb{N}^{2}
is given as follows:

(4.2)

\mathbf{P}\coloneqq\begin{cases}\{(i,j):1\leq i\leq p\text{ and }1\leq j\leq q\}&\text{ if $G_{\mathbb{R}}=\U(p,q)$},\\
\{(i,j):1\leq i\leq j\leq n\}&\text{ if $G_{\mathbb{R}}=\operatorname{Mp}(2n,\mathbb{R})$},\\
\{(i,j):1\leq i<j\leq n\}&\text{ if $G_{\mathbb{R}}=\operatorname{O}^{*}(2n)$}.\end{cases}

For
G_{\mathbb{R}}=\U(p,q)
or
\operatorname{O}^{*}(2n)
, we equip
\mathbf{P}
with the product order, whereby
(i,j)\leq(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
if and only if
i\leq i^{\prime}
and
j\leq j^{\prime}
.
For
G_{\mathbb{R}}=\operatorname{Mp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
, we reflect this partial order so that
(i,j)\leq(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
if and only if
i\leq i^{\prime}
and
j\geq j^{\prime}
.
In all cases, we will depict
\mathbf{P}
so that the minimal element is in the northwest corner; thus each element is covered by the element to its east and the element to its south.
Thus, adapting the terminology from Definition
4.1
, we observe that our depiction of
\mathbf{P}
is a
p\times q
rectangle, an
n
-staircase, and a shifted
(n-1)
-staircase, respectively:

G_{\mathbb{R}}=\U(p,q)
,
(p,q)=(8,12)

G_{\mathbb{R}}=\operatorname{Mp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
,
n=8

G_{\mathbb{R}}=\operatorname{O}^{*}(2n)
,
n=9

Remark 4.3
.

Recall the set
\Phi(\mathfrak{p}^{+})
from (
2.1
).
When
G_{\mathbb{R}}=\U(p,q)
or
\operatorname{O}^{*}(2n)
, the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
is simply laced, and we have an isomorphism of posets

\mathbf{P}\longrightarrow\Phi(\mathfrak{p}^{+})
given by

\begin{cases}(i,j)\longmapsto(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\leftarrow\scriptstyle\crcr\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\epsilon_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\leftarrow\scriptstyle\crcr\cr$\hfil\textstyle\epsilon_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\leftarrow\scriptscriptstyle\crcr\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\epsilon_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\leftarrow\scriptscriptstyle\crcr\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\epsilon_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}\mid-\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\rightarrow\scriptstyle\crcr\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\epsilon_{j}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\rightarrow\scriptstyle\crcr\cr$\hfil\textstyle\epsilon_{j}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\rightarrow\scriptscriptstyle\crcr\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\epsilon_{j}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\rightarrow\scriptscriptstyle\crcr\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\epsilon_{j}\hfil$\crcr}}})&\text{if }G_{\mathbb{R}}=\U(p,q),\\[8.61108pt]
(i,j)\longmapsto\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\leftarrow\scriptstyle\crcr\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\epsilon_{i}+\epsilon_{j}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\leftarrow\scriptstyle\crcr\cr$\hfil\textstyle\epsilon_{i}+\epsilon_{j}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\leftarrow\scriptscriptstyle\crcr\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\epsilon_{i}+\epsilon_{j}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\leftarrow\scriptscriptstyle\crcr\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\epsilon_{i}+\epsilon_{j}\hfil$\crcr}}}&\text{if }G_{\mathbb{R}}=\operatorname{O}^{*}(2n).\end{cases}

We further develop this observation in Section
7
.

Recall that a
chain
in
\mathbf{P}
is a totally ordered subset of
\mathbf{P}
.
An
antichain
is a subset of
\mathbf{P}
in which any two elements are incomparable.
The
width
of a subset
\mathbf{S}\subseteq\mathbf{P}
is the cardinality of the largest antichain contained in
\mathbf{S}
.
For a positive integer
k
, the
k
th order complex
on
\mathbf{P}
is the abstract simplicial complex

(4.3)

\Delta_{k}(\mathbf{P})\coloneqq\{\mathbf{S}\subseteq\mathbf{P}:\mathbf{S}\text{ has width at most $k$}\}.

A
facet
of
\Delta_{k}(\mathbf{P})
is an element of
\Delta_{k}(\mathbf{P})
that is maximal with respect to inclusion.
We denote the set of facets by

(4.4)

\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{k}\coloneqq\{\mathbf{F}:\text{$\mathbf{F}$ is a facet of $\Delta_{k}(\mathbf{P})$}\}.

Observe that for all three cases in (
4.2
), the width of
\mathbf{P}
equals the parameter
r
(see Table
2
).
Therefore, if
k\geq r
then
\Delta_{k}(\mathbf{P})
is just the power set of
\mathbf{P}
, and
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{k}=\{\mathbf{P}\}
.

Note that a saturated chain in
\mathbf{P}
is a
lattice path
, obtained by taking steps in the direction
(1,0)
or
(0,1)
.
Accordingly, in our diagrams the steps in a lattice path are either south or east.
If the starting point and endpoint coincide, then the lattice path contains only that single point.
Assuming
k\leq r
, it is well known that

(4.5)

\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{k}=\{\mathbf{F}:\mathbf{F}\text{ is a maximal union of $k$ nonintersecting lattice paths in $\mathbf{P}$}\}.

Note that in all three settings (still assuming
k\leq r
), there are certain points common to every
\mathbf{F}\in\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{k}
:

•

Let
G_{\mathbb{R}}=\U(p,q)
.
Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{k}\in\mathbf{P}
be the points lying on the
k
th antidiagonal
i+j=k+1
, listed from northeast to southwest.
Let
b_{1},\ldots,b_{k}\in\mathbf{P}
be obtained by translating
a_{1},\ldots,a_{k}
(respectively) by
(p-k,\>q-k)
.
Then every
\mathbf{F}
contains the disjoint union
\coprod_{t=1}^{k}(\mathbf{K}_{t}\sqcup\mathbf{M}_{t})
, where
\mathbf{K}_{t}
is the horizontal lattice path ending at
a_{t}
, and
\mathbf{M}_{t}
is the vertical lattice path starting at
b_{t}
.

•

Let
G_{\mathbb{R}}=\operatorname{Mp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
.
Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{k}\in\mathbf{P}
be the points lying on the
k
th antidiagonal, listed from northeast to southwest.
Then every
\mathbf{F}
contains the disjoint union
\coprod_{t=1}^{k}\mathbf{K}_{t}
, where
\mathbf{K}_{t}
is defined in the same way as the
\U(p,q)
case above.

•

Let
G_{\mathbb{R}}=\operatorname{O}^{*}(2n)
.
Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{k}\in\mathbf{P}
be the points lying on the
2k
th antidiagonal
i+j=2(k+1)
, listed from northeast to southwest.
Let
b_{1},\ldots,b_{k}\in\mathbf{P}
be obtained by translating
a_{1},\ldots,a_{k}
(respectively) by
(n-2k-1,\>n-2k-1)
(equivalently, reflecting about the line
i+j=n+1
).
Then every
\mathbf{F}
contains the disjoint union
\coprod_{t=1}^{k}(\mathbf{K}_{t}\sqcup\mathbf{M}_{t})
, defined in the same way as the
\U(p,q)
case above.

See the bottom row of Figure
1
, where we indicate the
a_{t}
’s (resp.,
b_{t}
’s) with enlarged dots in the northwest (resp., southeast) corner of
\mathbf{P}
.
The
\mathbf{K}_{t}
’s (resp.,
\mathbf{M}_{t}
’s) are the gray paths ending at the
a_{t}
’s (resp., starting at the
b_{t}
’s).
In the following definition, for each plane partition
P\in\mathcal{P}_{k}
we construct a subset
\Theta(P)\subseteq\mathbf{P}
.
