{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}&\end{array}

Figure 1
.
The bijection
\Theta:\mathcal{P}_{k}\longrightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{k}
in Definition
4.4
and Lemma
4.5
.

Definition 4.4
.

Let
P\in\mathcal{P}_{k}
, as in Definition
4.1
.
Recall that
k\geq r
implies
\mathcal{P}_{k}=\{\varnothing\}
; in this case, we define
\Theta(\varnothing)\coloneqq\mathbf{P}
.
If
k<r
, then construct
\Theta(P)\subseteq\mathbf{P}
as follows:

•

For
1\leq t\leq k
:

–

Align the upper-left corner of
P
with the point
a_{t}
.

–

Draw the lattice path
\mathbf{L}_{t}\subseteq\mathbf{P}
from
a_{t}
to
b_{t}
(or if
H(k)=\operatorname{O}_{k}
, from
a_{t}
to the main antidiagonal of
\mathbf{P}
) such that
\mathbf{L}_{t}
is the southwest boundary of the subdiagram
P_{t}
containing those boxes with entries strictly greater than
k-t
.

•

Define
\Theta(P)\coloneqq\coprod_{t=1}^{k}\mathbf{N}_{t}
, where
\mathbf{N}_{t}\coloneqq\mathbf{K}_{t}\cup\mathbf{L}_{t}\cup\mathbf{M}_{t}
;
if
G_{\mathbb{R}}=\operatorname{Mp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
, then
\mathbf{N}_{t}\coloneqq\mathbf{K}_{t}\cup\mathbf{L}_{t}
.

For examples of the map
P\mapsto\Theta(P)
in Definition
4.4
, see the top and bottom row of Figure
1
;
in the bottom row, the paths
\mathbf{L}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{L}_{k}
(from northeast to southwest) are drawn in black.
In Figure
1
, we also show the three-dimensional depiction of
P
, which makes it easy to trace out
\mathbf{L}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{L}_{k}
(from top to bottom).

The following lemma is closely related to the bijections given by Krattenthaler
[
30
]
, who used two-row arrays (equivalently, nonnegative integer matrices) instead of plane partitions.
(See in particular Propositions 28, 30, and 33 in
[
30
]
, corresponding to our cases
G_{\mathbb{R}}=\U(p,q)
,
\operatorname{O}^{*}(2n)
, and
\operatorname{Mp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
, respectively.)

Lemma 4.5
.

The map
P\mapsto\Theta(P)
in Definition
4.4
gives a bijection
\Theta:\mathcal{P}_{k}\longrightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{k}
.

Proof.

(Throughout the proof, suppress any mention of
b_{t}
and
\mathbf{M}_{t}
in the
G_{\mathbb{R}}=\operatorname{Mp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
case.)
By construction,
\Theta(P)
is a family of
k
lattice paths in
\mathbf{P}
, since each
\mathbf{K}_{t}
ends at the starting point
a_{t}
of
\mathbf{L}_{t}
, which ends at the starting point
b_{t}
of
\mathbf{M}_{t}
.
Since
P_{1}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq P_{k}
, the southwest boundary of
P_{t+1}
lies weakly southwest of the southwest boundary of
P_{t}
;
thus, since the starting point
a_{t+1}
of
\mathbf{L}_{t+1}
is strictly southwest of the starting point
a_{t}
of
\mathbf{L}_{t}
, the paths
\mathbf{L}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{L}_{t}
are indeed nonintersecting.
This justifies the disjoint union
\coprod_{t}\mathbf{N}_{t}
written in the last step of Definition
4.4
.
Maximality follows immediately from the starting and ending points of the
\mathbf{N}_{t}
’s.
Hence by (
4.5
), we have
\Theta(P)\in\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{k}
.
It is clear that distinct plane partitions in
\mathcal{P}_{k}
yield distinct families
\{\mathbf{L}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{L}_{k}\}
, so
\Theta
is one-to-one.

In the other direction, given
\mathbf{F}\in\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{k}
, one can decompose
\mathbf{F}
into a maximal union of
k
nonintersecting lattice paths
\mathbf{N}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{N}_{k}
, by (
4.5
), such that each
\mathbf{N}_{t}
contains
\mathbf{K}_{t}
and
\mathbf{M}_{t}
.
This leaves nonintersecting lattice paths
\mathbf{L}_{t}
from
a_{t}
to
b_{t}
, for
1\leq t\leq k
.
Now by superimposing these paths
\mathbf{L}_{t}
so that they all start in the upper-left corner of the diagram
D_{k}
, we obtain the plane partition
\Theta^{-1}(\mathbf{F})\in\mathcal{P}_{k}
as follows:
the entry in each box of
D_{k}
equals the number of
\mathbf{L}_{t}
’s lying to the southwest of that box.
Hence
\Theta
is onto, which completes the proof.
∎

5.
Stanley decompositions of unitary highest weight modules

This section is a self-contained summary of the main result in
[
14
]
; see Lemma
5.5
below.
This lemma plays a key role in proving our main result in Section
6
.

5.1.
Multiplicity and Stanley decompositions

We recall some fundamental results from commutative algebra concerning finitely generated graded modules over polynomial rings.

Definition 5.1
.

Let
M=\bigoplus_{n=0}^{\infty}M_{n}
be a finitely generated graded
S
-module with Krull dimension
d
, where
S
is a polynomial ring over
\mathbb{C}
in determinates of degree 1.
The
Hilbert–Poincaré series
of
M
is the formal power series

H_{M}(t)\coloneqq\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(\dim M_{n})t^{n}.

Let
M
be as in Definition
5.1
.
By the Hilbert–Serre theorem (see also
[
4
]
*Cor. 4.1.8), there exists a unique
Q_{M}(t)\in\mathbb{Z}[t]
with
Q(1)\neq 0
such that

(5.1)

H_{M}(t)=\frac{Q_{M}(t)}{(1-t)^{d}}.

Definition 5.2
.

Let
M
be as in Definition
5.1
, with reduced Hilbert series given by (
5.1
).
The
multiplicity
of
M
is the positive integer
e(M)\coloneqq Q_{M}(1)
.

The multiplicity
e(M)
equals
(d-1)!
times the leading coefficient of the Hilbert polynomial
P_{M}(t)
; see the exposition in
[
4
]
, particularly page 150 and Proposition 4.1.9.
Comparing Definition
5.2
with (
1.1
), we see that multiplicity is synonymous with Bernstein degree, in the following sense.
Given a good filtration
\{X_{n}\}
in the context of (
1.1
), the graded object

\operatorname{gr}X\coloneqq\bigoplus_{n=0}^{\infty}X_{n}/X_{n-1}

is a graded module over
S(\mathfrak{g})
, and following
[
33
]
*Def. 1.3, we define

(5.2)

\operatorname{Deg}X\coloneqq e(\operatorname{gr}X).

Definition 5.3

(see
[
5
]
*Def. 2.1)
.

Let
M
be as in Definition
5.1
.
A
Stanley decomposition
of
M
is a finite family
(S_{i},\eta_{i})_{i\in\mathcal{I}}

where
\eta_{i}\in M
is homogeneous, and
S_{i}
is a graded
\mathbb{C}
-algebra retract of
S
such that
S_{i}\cap\operatorname{Ann}\eta_{i}=0
, and

(5.3)

M=\bigoplus_{i\in\mathcal{I}}S_{i}\eta_{i}

as a graded vector space.
Each
S_{i}\eta_{i}
in (
5.3
) is called the
Stanley space
corresponding to
i\in\mathcal{I}
.

By
[
23
]
*Lemma 1.1, any module
M
satisfying the hypotheses of Definition
5.1
admits a Stanley decomposition.
(The terminology refers to Stanley’s work
[
39
]
on commutative monoids.)
Note that a Stanley decomposition (
5.3
) yields the following closed form for the Hilbert series:

(5.4)

H_{M}(t)=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\frac{t^{\deg\eta_{i}}}{(1-t)^{{\rm Kdim}(S_{i})}},

where
{\rm Kdim}
denotes the Krull dimension.
Comparing this with the reduced form of the Hilbert series in (
5.1
), we make the following key observation.

Lemma 5.4

(
[
26
]
*p. 1018)
.

Let
M
be as in Definition
5.1
, and let
(S_{i},\eta_{i})_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
be a Stanley decomposition of
M
.
Then

d\coloneqq{\rm Kdim}(M)=\max_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\{{\rm Kdim}(S_{i})\}.

Furthermore, defining

\widehat{\mathcal{I}}\coloneqq\{i\in\mathcal{I}:{\rm Kdim}(S_{i})=d\},

we have

e(M)=\#\widehat{\mathcal{I}}.

5.2.
Stanley decompositions of unitary highest weight modules

In this paper, the role of
M
in (
5.3
) is played by the unitary highest weight modules
L_{\lambda(\sigma)}
arising in the dual pair setting (
2.7
).
The role of
S
is played by the ring defined in (
2.1
), namely

S=\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{p}^{+}]=\mathbb{C}[z_{ij}:(i,j)\in\mathbf{P}],

where
\mathbf{P}
is the poset in (
4.2
).
Each
L_{\lambda(\sigma)}
has the structure of a graded
S
-module, where the grading is induced from the degree filtration coming from
U(\mathfrak{g})
, normalized so that the weight space corresponding to the highest weight
\lambda
has degree 0.
In particular, the degree of the
\mu
-weight space is
(\lambda-\mu)(h_{0})
, where
h_{0}\in\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{k})
is the distinguished element described at the beginning of Section
2.1
.
Thus by (
5.2
), the Bernstein degree of
L_{\lambda(\sigma)}
is synonymous with its multiplicity as a graded
S
-module:

(5.5)

\operatorname{Deg}L_{\lambda(\sigma)}=e(L_{\lambda(\sigma)}).

In previous work
[
14
]
, we obtained Stanley decompositions of all modules
L_{\lambda(\sigma)}
, for the groups
\U(p,q)
and
\operatorname{O}^{*}(2n)
.
The role of the index set
\mathcal{I}
in (
5.3
) is played by a set
\mathcal{J}_{k}(\sigma)
consisting of combinatorial objects we call
jellyfish of shape
\sigma
.
Note that in
[
14
]
, we stated this result in terms of the modules of covariants
M_{\sigma}
of the classical groups
H(k)
; see (
2.3
)–(
2.4
) and (
2.6
) above.
Recall from (
2.7
) that (upon viewing
M_{\sigma}
as the
\mathfrak{k}
-module
M_{\sigma}^{\text{class.}}\otimes F_{-kc\zeta}
) we have
M_{\sigma}\cong L_{\lambda}(\sigma)
as
(\mathfrak{g},K)
-modules;
moreover, in
[
14
]
we observed that, upon shifting the natural grading on
M_{\sigma}
via
d\mapsto(d-|\sigma|)/2
, we also have
M_{\sigma}\cong L_{\lambda(\sigma)}
as graded
S
-modules.
Therefore, in stating Lemma
5.5
below (where we record our main result from
[
14
]
), we apply to
L_{\lambda(\sigma)}
our Stanley decompositions of
M_{\sigma}
.
After the lemma, we will explain the notation in detail.
(In light of Lemma
5.4
, however, we will omit here those details that do not pertain to the Krull dimension of the Stanley spaces; we refer the reader to the exposition in
[
14
]
*§3.)

Lemma 5.5

(
[
14
]
*Thm. 3.2)
.

Assume the setting in Table
2
where
G_{\mathbb{R}}=\U(p,q)
or
\operatorname{O}^{*}(2n)
.

If
k<s
, then the graded
S
-module
L_{\lambda(\sigma)}
admits a Stanley decomposition

(5.6)

L_{\lambda(\sigma)}\cong M_{\sigma}=\bigoplus_{(T,\mathbf{F})\in\mathcal{J}_{k}(\sigma)}\mathbb{C}[z_{ij}:(i,j)\in\mathbf{F}]\left(\prod_{(i,j)\in{\rm cor}(\mathbf{F})}\hskip-8.61108ptz_{ij}\right)\varphi_{T},

where

•

\mathcal{J}_{k}(\sigma)
is the set of jellyfish to be defined below (Definition
5.6
);

•

each
T
is a tableau in the set
\mathcal{T}(\sigma)
from Definition
3.1
;

•

each
\mathbf{F}
is a (not necessarily maximal) union of
k
nonintersecting lattice paths in
\mathbf{P}
(see (
4.2
)), and thus is an element of the complex
\Delta_{k}(\mathbf{P})
defined above in (
4.3
); see also (
5.8
) below;

•

{\rm cor}(\mathbf{F})
is the set of “corners” of the lattice paths determining
\mathbf{F}
, defined in
[
14
]
*eqn. (3.4);
the details are not pertinent to the result in the present paper.

•

\varphi_{T}
is an
H(k)
-equivariant map canonically induced by
T
, and defined in
[
14
]
*eqn. (2.12);
the details are not pertinent to the result in the present paper.

If
k\geq s
, then
L_{\lambda(\sigma)}
is a free
S
-module with Stanley decomposition

(5.7)

L_{\lambda(\sigma)}\cong M_{\sigma}=\bigoplus_{T\in\mathcal{T}(\sigma)}S\,\varphi_{T}.

5.3.
Jellyfish: uniform overview

Sections
5.3
–
5.5
will be a summary of our exposition in
[
14
]
*§3.
In this subsection, we give a uniform definition of the set
\mathcal{J}_{k}(\sigma)
parametrizing the Stanley spaces in (
5.6
).
Then in the following two subsections, we will give the details specific to
G_{\mathbb{R}}=\operatorname{O}^{*}(2n)
and
\U(p,q)
, respectively;
we choose this order because the combinatorial details in the
\operatorname{O}^{*}(2n)
case are considerably more straightforward.

From now on, assume that
k<s
.
We recall the set
\mathbf{P}
from (
4.2
).
We will define a region
\mathbf{A}\subseteq\mathbf{P}
lying above a certain antidiagonal.
Then
\delta(\mathbf{A})
or
\delta(\mathbf{P})
will denote the set of points lying on a certain boundary of
\mathbf{A}
or
\mathbf{P}
, respectively.
We define the set

(5.8)

\mathcal{F}_{k}\coloneqq\Big\{\big(\mathbf{A}\setminus\delta(\mathbf{A})\big)\sqcup\coprod_{t=1}^{k}\mathbf{L}_{t}:\text{each $\mathbf{L}_{t}$ is a lattice path from $\delta(\mathbf{A})$ to $\delta(\mathbf{P})$}\Big\},

which contains the facet set
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{k}
defined above in (
4.4
).
Given
\mathbf{F}\in\mathcal{F}_{k}
, a
corner
of
\mathbf{F}
is an east-to-south turn in one of its lattice paths, such that the string of points to its southwest contains at least one point not in
\mathbf{F}
.
(Although in the
\U(p,q)
case the decomposition of
\mathbf{F}
into lattice paths
\mathbf{L}_{t}
is in general not unique, nonetheless the corners of
\mathbf{F}
are well defined.)
The symbol
{\rm cor}(\mathbf{F})
in Lemma
5.5
denotes the set of corners of
\mathbf{F}
;
this will not, however, be relevant to the main result in this paper.

We define the collection

(5.9)

\mathcal{E}_{k}\coloneqq\left\{\{\text{endpoint of $\mathbf{L}_{t}$}\}_{t=1}^{k}:\begin{array}[]{l}\{\mathbf{L}_{t}\}_{t=1}^{k}\text{ is a family of nonintersecting}\\
\text{lattice paths from $\delta(\mathbf{A})$ to $\delta(\mathbf{P})$}\end{array}\right\},

consisting of all possible sets
\mathbf{E}\subseteq\delta(\mathbf{P})
of endpoints for the lattice paths appearing in (
5.8
).
We then partition
\mathcal{F}_{k}
and
\mathcal{T}(\sigma)
(from Definition
3.1
) into subsets
\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{E}}
and
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{E}}(\sigma)
as follows.

For
\mathbf{E}\in\mathcal{E}
, define

(5.10)

\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{E}}\coloneqq\left\{\mathbf{F}\in\mathcal{F}_{k}:\begin{array}[]{l}\mathbf{F}=(\mathbf{A}\setminus\delta(\mathbf{A}))\sqcup\coprod_{t=1}^{k}\mathbf{L}_{t},\\
\text{where each $\mathbf{L}_{t}$ is a lattice path from $\delta(\mathbf{A})$ to $\mathbf{E}$}\end{array}\right\}.

For
G_{\mathbb{R}}=\operatorname{O}^{*}(2n)
we will have
\mathcal{F}_{k}=\coprod_{\mathbf{E}\in\mathcal{E}}\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{E}}
, but for
G_{\mathbb{R}}=\U(p,q)
it is possible that
\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{E}}=\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{E}^{\prime}}
.
Note that
\#\mathbf{F}
is determined by the endpoints of its lattice paths;
hence, for each
\mathbf{E}\in\mathcal{E}
, every element of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{E}}
has the same cardinality.
In light of Lemma
5.4
, we define

(5.11)

\displaystyle\begin{split}d_{k}&\coloneqq\max_{\mathbf{F}\in\mathcal{F}_{k}}\{\#\mathbf{F}\},\\
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{k}&\coloneqq\{\mathbf{F}\in\mathcal{F}_{k}:\#\mathbf{F}=d_{k}\},\\
\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{k}&\coloneqq\{\mathbf{E}\in\mathcal{E}_{k}:\text{if }\mathbf{F}\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{E}}\text{ then }\mathbf{F}\in\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{k}\}.\end{split}

Note that this definition of
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{k}
is equivalent to the one given in (
4.4
).

Given
\sigma\in\widehat{H}(k)
, and recalling the tableau set
\mathcal{T}(\sigma)
from Definition
3.1
, we will define a map

(5.12)

{\rm end}:\mathcal{T}(\sigma)\longrightarrow\mathcal{E}_{k},

so that (very roughly speaking)
{\rm end}(T)
is the set of endpoints most closely aligning with the initial column of
T
.
From now on, we write
T_{t,1}
to denote the entry of
T
in row
t
and column
1
.
For each
\mathbf{E}\in\mathcal{E}_{k}
, we define the fiber

(5.13)

\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{E}}(\sigma)\coloneqq\{T\in\mathcal{T}(\sigma):{\rm end}(T)=\mathbf{E}\}.

Clearly
\mathcal{T}(\sigma)=\coprod_{\mathbf{E}\in\mathcal{E}}T_{\mathbf{E}}(\sigma)
, although we may have
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{E}}(\sigma)=\varnothing
for certain
\mathbf{E}\in\mathcal{E}_{k}
.

Definition 5.6
.

Let
G_{\mathbb{R}}=\U(p,q)
or
\operatorname{O}^{*}(2n)
, with
k<s
and
\sigma\in\widehat{H}(k)
, as in Table
2
.
A
jellyfish of shape
\sigma
is an element of the set

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{k}(\sigma)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\coprod_{\mathbf{E}\in\mathcal{E}_{k}}\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{E}}(\sigma)\times\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{E}},

with
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{E}}(\sigma)
and
\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{E}}
as in (
5.13
) and (
5.10
), and with case-by-case details to be described below.
Recalling the set
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{k}
from (
5.11
), we say a jellyfish is
maximal
if it belongs to the subset

\widehat{\mathcal{J}}_{k}(\sigma)\coloneqq\Big\{(T,\mathbf{F})\in\mathcal{J}_{k}(\sigma):\mathbf{F}\in\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{k}\Big\}.

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
G_{\mathbb{R}}=\U(p,q)
or
\operatorname{O}^{*}(2n)
, with
k<s
and
\sigma\in\widehat{H}(k)
, as in Table
2
.
We have

\operatorname{Deg}L_{\lambda(\sigma)}=\#\widehat{\mathcal{J}}_{k}(\sigma).

Proof.

Consider the Stanley decomposition of
L_{\lambda(\sigma)}
in (
5.6
), which is the
k<s
case of Lemma
5.5
.
Each
(T,\mathbf{F})\in\mathcal{J}_{k}(\sigma)
corresponds to the Stanley space
S_{(T,\mathbf{F})}\eta_{(T,\mathbf{F})}
, where
S_{(T,\mathbf{F})}=\mathbb{C}[z_{ij}:(i,j)\in\mathbf{F}]
has Krull dimension equal to
\#\mathbf{F}
.
Now we can apply Lemma
5.4
, where the role of
M
is played by
L_{\lambda(\sigma)}
, the role of
d
is played by
d_{k}
from (
5.11
), the role of
\mathcal{I}
is played by
\mathcal{J}_{k}(\sigma)
, and the role of
\widehat{\mathcal{I}}
is played by
\widehat{\mathcal{J}}_{k}(\sigma)
from Definition
5.6
.
In this special case, Lemma
5.4
yields

e\!\left(L_{\lambda(\sigma)}\right)=\#\widehat{\mathcal{J}}_{k}(\sigma).

The result follows from (
5.5
).
∎

5.4.
Details for
\operatorname{O}^{*}(2n)

Let
G_{\mathbb{R}}=\operatorname{O}^{*}(2n)
.
Then
\mathbf{P}=\{(i,j):1\leq i<j\leq n\}
is depicted as an
(n-1)
-staircase, with
(1,2)
in the northwest corner, and
(n-1,n)
in the southeast corner.
Let

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{(i,j)\in\mathbf{P}:i+j\leq 2(k+1)\},

\displaystyle\delta(\mathbf{A})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{(i,j)\in\mathbf{A}:i+j=2(k+1)\text{ or }j=n\},

\displaystyle\delta(\mathbf{P})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{(i,j)\in\mathbf{P}:j=n\}.

Note that
\delta
denotes the eastern boundary.
Explicitly, we have

(5.14)

\delta(\mathbf{A})=\{(t,b_{t})\}_{t=1}^{k},\quad\text{where}\quad b_{t}=\begin{cases}n&\text{if }t<2(k+1)-n,\\
2(k+1)-t&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Referring to (
5.8
), we observe that each
\mathbf{F}\in\mathcal{F}_{k}
admits a unique decomposition
\mathbf{F}=(\mathbf{A}\setminus\delta(\mathbf{A}))\sqcup\coprod_{t=1}^{k}\mathbf{L}_{t}
, where each
\mathbf{L}_{t}
is a southeast lattice path starting at
(t,b_{t})
and ending in
\delta(\mathbf{P})
.
In (
5.15
) below, where
n=11
, we show examples of an element
\mathbf{F}\in\mathcal{F}_{k}
for two different values of
k
.
Following (
5.8
), we shade the points in
\mathbf{F}
, and we draw squares to indicate the elements of
{\rm cor}(\mathbf{F})
:

(5.15)

k=4

k=7

Let
\mathbf{E}=\{(i_{t},n)\}_{t=1}^{k}\in\mathcal{E}_{k}
where the
i_{t}
’s are listed in increasing order.
By (
5.10
), if
\mathbf{F}\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{E}}
, then each
\mathbf{L}_{t}
is a lattice path from
(t,b_{t})
to
(i_{t},n)
.
Hence

(5.16)

\#\mathbf{F}=\#(\mathbf{A}\setminus\delta(\mathbf{A}))+\sum_{t=1}^{k}\#\mathbf{L}_{t},\quad\text{where}\quad\#\mathbf{L}_{t}=(i_{t}-t)+(n-b_{t})+1.

The
b_{t}
’s in (
5.14
) are independent of
\mathbf{F}
, and thus
\#\mathbf{F}
depends only on the sum
\sum_{t=1}^{k}i_{t}
.
Crucially, due to the nonintersecting property of the lattice paths, the maximum attainable value
\hat{\imath}_{t}
for
i_{t}
is given by

(5.17)

\hat{\imath}_{t}=\begin{cases}t&\text{if }t<2(k+1)-n,\\
n+2(t-k)-1&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Note that the topmost case in (
5.17
)
corresponds to those
t
’s (if any) for which
\mathbf{L}_{t}=\{(t,b_{t})\}
is necessarily a singleton;
for example, in the right-hand example in (
5.15
), this occurs for
t<2(k+1)-n=5
, and indeed the four topmost lattice paths are singletons.
Putting
\widehat{\mathbf{E}}\coloneqq\{(\hat{\imath}_{t},n)\}_{t=1}^{k}
, it follows from the above discussion that
\mathbf{F}
attains the maximum possible cardinality
d_{k}
if and only if
\mathbf{F}\in\mathcal{F}_{\widehat{\mathbf{E}}}
;
that is, recalling the sets
\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{k}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{k}
defined in (
5.11
),

(5.18)

\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{k}=\{\widehat{\mathbf{E}}\},\text{ and therefore }\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{k}=\mathcal{F}_{\widehat{\mathbf{E}}}.

We now define the “end” map (
5.12
) as follows, for
T\in\mathcal{T}(\sigma)=\SSYT(\sigma,n)
:

(5.19)

{\rm end}(T)\coloneqq\{(i_{t},n)\}_{t=1}^{k},\quad\text{where }i_{t}=\min\{T_{t,1},\>\hat{\imath}_{t}\}.

Since
T_{t,1}
is undefined for
t>\ell(\sigma)
, in this range the equation (
5.19
) reduces to
i_{t}=\hat{\imath}_{t}
.
In the running examples below, for a given tableau
T
we indicate
{\rm end}(T)
by highlighting its elements in red inside the vertical strip
\delta(\mathbf{P})
:

{\rm end}(T)

5.5.
Details for
\U(p,q)

Let
G_{\mathbb{R}}=\U(p,q)
.
Then
\mathbf{P}=\{(i,j):1\leq i\leq p,\>1\leq j\leq q\}
is a
p\times q
grid, with
(1,1)
in the northwest corner, and
(p,q)
in the southwest corner.
Let

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{(i,j)\in\mathbf{P}:i+j\leq k+1\},

\displaystyle\delta(\mathbf{A})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{(i,j)\in\mathbf{A}:i+j=k+1\text{ or }i=p\text{ or }j=q\},

\displaystyle\delta(\mathbf{P})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{(i,j)\in\mathbf{P}:i=p\text{ or }j=q\}.

Note that
\delta
denotes the southeastern boundary.
Given
\sigma\in\widehat{H}(k)
, it will be useful to define

(5.20)

\displaystyle\begin{split}k^{+}&\coloneqq\max\{\ell(\sigma^{+}),\>k-p\},\\
k^{-}&\coloneqq k-k^{+},\end{split}

so that
k=k^{+}+k^{-}
.
The purpose of (
5.20
) is to divide
\delta(\mathbf{A})
into two sides:
due to the upcoming definition of
{\rm end}(T)
in (
5.26
), it will turn out that
(T,\mathbf{F})\in\mathcal{J}_{k}(\sigma)
only if the
k^{+}
many southernmost lattice paths in
\mathbf{F}
have endpoints along the southern edge of
\delta(\mathbf{P})
, and the remaining
k^{-}
many easternmost lattice paths have endpoints along the eastern edge of
\delta(\mathbf{P})
.
In particular, if
k-p
is positive, then it equals the number of starting points along the southern edge of
\delta(\mathbf{A})\cap\delta(\mathbf{P})
that are necessarily their own endpoints.
Explicitly, then, we have
\delta(\mathbf{A})=\{(a_{u},u)\}_{u=1}^{k^{+}}\sqcup\{(t,b_{t})\}_{t=1}^{k^{-}}
, where

(5.21)

a_{u}=\begin{cases}p&\text{if }u\leq k-p,\\
k-u+1&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}\qquad\text{and}\qquad b_{t}=\begin{cases}q&\text{if }t\leq k-q,\\
k-t+1&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

By (
5.8
), for each
\mathbf{F}\in\mathcal{F}_{k}
we have
\mathbf{F}=(\mathbf{A}\setminus\delta(\mathbf{A}))\sqcup\mathbf{L}
, for a (not necessarily unique) disjoint union
\mathbf{L}=\coprod_{u=1}^{k^{+}}\mathbf{L}^{+}_{u}\sqcup\coprod_{t=1}^{k^{-}}\mathbf{L}^{-}_{t}
, where each
\mathbf{L}^{+}_{u}
is a southeast lattice path from
(a_{u},u)
to
\delta(\mathbf{P})
, and
\mathbf{L}^{-}_{t}
is a southeast lattice path from
(t,b_{t})
to
\delta(\mathbf{P})
.
Below, for
(p,q)=(7,10)
, we show examples of an element
\mathbf{F}\in\mathcal{F}_{k}
for three different values of
k
:

k=4

k=8

k=12

Let
\mathbf{E}=\{(p,j_{u})\}_{u=1}^{k^{+}}\sqcup\{(i_{t},q)\}_{t=1}^{k}\in\mathcal{E}_{k}
where the
j_{u}
’s and
i_{t}
’s are listed in increasing order.
By (
5.10
), if
\mathbf{F}\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{E}}
, then each
\mathbf{L}^{+}_{u}
is a lattice path from
(a_{u},u)
to
(p,j_{u})
, and each
\mathbf{L}^{-}_{t}
is a lattice path from
(t,b_{t})
to
(i_{t},q)
.
By same argument as in (
5.16
),
\#\mathbf{F}
depends only on the sum
\sum_{u}j_{u}+\sum_{t}i_{t}
.
Crucially, due to the nonintersecting condition on the lattice paths
\mathbf{L}^{+}_{u}
and
\mathbf{L}^{-}_{t}
, each
\mathbf{E}\in\mathcal{E}_{k}
has the following property:
the
\ell\times\ell
square in the lower-right corner of
\mathbf{P}
contains at most
\ell
points in
\mathbf{E}
, for all
1\leq\ell\leq\min\{k,r\}
.
Thus we have the tight inequalities

(5.22)

\#\{u:j_{u}>q-\ell\}+\#\{t:i_{t}>p-\ell\}\leq\ell\quad\text{for all }1\leq\ell\leq\min\{k,r\}.

It follows that
\mathbf{F}
attains the maximum possible cardinality
d_{k}
if and only if the inequalities (
5.22
) are all tight at
\mathbf{E}
, that is,
\#\{u:j_{u}>q-\ell\}+\#\{t:i_{t}>p-\ell\}=\ell
for all
1\leq\ell\leq\min\{k,r\}
.
Equivalently, since
\#\mathbf{E}=k
, we have that
\#\mathbf{F}=d_{k}
if and only if

(5.23)

\#\{u:j_{u}\leq q-\ell\}+\#\{t:i_{t}\leq p-\ell\}=k-\ell\quad\text{for all $1\leq\ell\leq\min\{k,r\}$},

that is, if and only if
\mathbf{F}
contains the isosceles right triangle in the southeastern corner of
\mathbf{P}
with side length
\min\{k,r\}
.
Thus by (
5.11
),

(5.24)

\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{k}=\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{E}}\text{ if and only if $\mathbf{E}\in\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{k}=\{\mathbf{E}\in\mathcal{E}_{k}:\mathbf{E}\text{ satisfies }\eqref{E max condition GL}\}$}.

Observe from (
5.23
) that upon fixing the
j_{u}
’s, the maximum attainable value
\hat{\imath}_{t}
of
i_{t}
is given by
\hat{\imath}_{t}=t
if
t<k-q
,
while if
t\geq k-q
, then the
\hat{\imath}_{t}
’s are the largest elements
\hat{\imath}_{\min\{1,\>k-q\}}<\cdots<\hat{\imath}_{k^{-}}
of the set

(5.25)

\Big\{i\in[p]:q-p+i\notin\{j_{u}\}_{u=1}^{k^{+}}\Big\}.

In other words, for
\min\{1,k-q\}\leq t\leq k^{-}
, the
\hat{\imath}_{t}
’s are the distinct elements chosen from
[p]
to be as large as possible without violating (
5.22
); see the example (
5.27
) below.

We define the “end” map (
5.12
) as follows, for
T=(T^{+},T^{-})\in\mathcal{T}(\sigma)=\SSYT(\sigma^{+},q)\times\SSYT(\sigma^{-},p)
:

{\rm end}(T)\coloneqq\{(p,j_{u})\}_{u=1}^{k^{+}}\sqcup\{(i_{t},q)\}_{t=1}^{k^{-}},

where

(5.26)

j_{u}=\begin{cases}u&\text{if }u\leq k-p,\\
T^{+}_{u,1}&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}\qquad\text{and}\qquad i_{t}=\min\{T^{-}_{t,1},\>\hat{\imath}_{t}\}.

Since
T^{-}_{t,1}
is undefined for
t>\ell(\sigma^{-})
, in this range the right-hand side of (
5.26
) reduces to
i_{t}=\hat{\imath}_{t}
.
As an example below, we indicate
{\rm end}(T)
by highlighting its elements in red inside
\delta(\mathbf{P})
:

(5.27)

\begin{aligned} T&=\left(\>\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to8.4pt{\vss\hbox to8.00006pt{\hss$\scriptstyle 3$\hss}\vss}\kern-8.4pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-8.4pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\hrule \hat{\imath}_{1}\rightarrow
\hat{\imath}_{2}\rightarrow
\hat{\imath}_{3}\rightarrow
\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to8.4pt{\vss\hbox to8.00006pt{\hss$\scriptstyle 1$\hss}\vss}\kern-8.4pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-8.4pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr}\kern 345.4pt}}$}\leftarrow T^{-}_{1,1}

\scriptstyle 3

\uparrow
T^{+}_{1,1}

\scriptstyle 4

\scriptstyle 7

\scriptstyle 9

\scriptstyle 10

\uparrow
T^{+}_{5,1}
{\rm end}(T)

The dashed lines in (
5.27
) are meant to illustrate the definition of the
\hat{\imath}_{t}
’s in (
5.25
).
Specifically, on the southern edge of
\delta(\mathbf{P})
, take the easternmost points that are
not
in the set
\{(p,j_{u})\}_{u=1}^{k^{+}}
; then the
\hat{\imath}_{t}
’s are their corresponding points along the eastern edge of
\delta(\mathbf{P})
.

6.
Main result

We recall the sets
\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)
and
\mathcal{P}_{k}
from Definitions
3.1
and
4.1
, along with the facet set
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{k}
from (
4.4
), and the map
\Theta:\mathcal{P}_{k}\longrightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{k}
from Definition
4.4
.
We also recall the set
\widehat{\mathcal{J}}_{k}(\sigma)
of maximal jellyfish in Definition
5.6
.

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
G_{\mathbb{R}}=\U(p,q)
or
\operatorname{O}^{*}(2n)
, where
k<s
(see Table
2
).
We have a bijection

{\rm id}\times\Theta:\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)\times\mathcal{P}_{k}\longrightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{J}}_{k}(\sigma).

Proof.

By Lemma
4.5
,
{\rm id}\times\Theta
is a bijection from
\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)\times\mathcal{P}_{k}
to
\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)\times\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{k}
.
Hence it suffices to prove that

(6.1)

\widehat{\mathcal{J}}_{k}(\sigma)=\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)\times\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{k}.

Starting from Definition
5.6
, we have

(6.2)

\widehat{\mathcal{J}}_{k}(\sigma)\coloneqq\{(T,\mathbf{F})\in\mathcal{J}_{k}(\sigma):\mathbf{F}\in\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{k}\}=\coprod_{\mathbf{E}\in\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{k}}\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{E}}(\sigma)\times\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{E}},

where the second equality follows from (
5.11
) and Definition
5.6
.

First let
G_{\mathbb{R}}=\operatorname{O}^{*}(2n)
.
By (
5.18
), we can rewrite (
6.2
) as

(6.3)

\widehat{\mathcal{J}}_{k}(\sigma)=\mathcal{T}_{\widehat{\mathbf{E}}}\times\mathcal{F}_{\widehat{\mathbf{E}}}=\mathcal{T}_{\widehat{\mathbf{E}}}\times\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{k}.

By (
5.13
),
T\in\mathcal{T}_{\widehat{\mathbf{E}}}
if and only if
{\rm end}(T)=\widehat{\mathbf{E}}=\{(\hat{\imath}_{t},n)\}_{t=1}^{k}
, with the
\hat{\imath}_{t}
’s defined in (
5.17
).
In turn, by (
5.19
),
{\rm end}(T)=\widehat{\mathbf{E}}
if and only if
T_{t,1}\geq\hat{\imath}_{t}
for all
1\leq t\leq\ell(\sigma)
.
This is automatic for
t<2(k+1)-n
, that is for
t\leq 2k-n+1
, so we need only check that
T_{t,1}\geq\hat{\imath}_{t}
for all
\max\{0,2k-n+1\}<j\leq\ell(\sigma)
.
But by comparing this and (
5.17
) with Lemma
3.5
(3), where
j
plays the role of
t
, we conclude that
T\in\mathcal{T}_{\widehat{\mathbf{E}}}
if and only if
T\in\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)
.
Hence
\mathcal{T}_{\widehat{\mathbf{E}}}=\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)
, and so by (
6.3
) we obtain (
6.1
), which completes the proof for
\operatorname{O}^{*}(2n)
.

Similarly, let
G_{\mathbb{R}}=\U(p,q)
.
By (
5.24
), we can rewrite (
6.2
) as

(6.4)

\widehat{\mathcal{J}}_{k}(\sigma)=\Bigg(\coprod_{\mathbf{E}\in\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{k}}\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{E}}(\sigma)\Bigg)\times\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{k}.

By (
5.13
),
T\in\coprod_{\mathbf{E}\in\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{k}}\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{E}}(\sigma)
if and only if
{\rm end}(T)\in\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{k}
.
In turn, by (
5.24
),
{\rm end}(T)\in\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{k}
if and only if
{\rm end}(T)=\{(p,j_{u})\}_{u=1}^{k^{+}}\sqcup\{(i_{t},q)\}_{t=1}^{k^{-}}
satisfies (
5.23
), which we reproduce below:

\#\{u:j_{u}\leq q-\ell\}+\#\{t:i_{t}\leq p-\ell\}=k-\ell\quad\text{for all $1\leq\ell\leq\min\{k,r\}$}.

By (
5.26
), each
T^{+}_{u,1}\geq j_{u}
and
T^{-}_{t,1}\geq i_{t}
, and therefore, we have
{\rm end}(T)\in\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{k}
if and only if

\#\{u:T^{+}_{u,1}\leq q-\ell\}+\#\{t:T^{-}_{t,1}\leq p-\ell\}\leq k-\ell\quad\text{for all $1\leq\ell\leq\min\{k,r\}$},

if and only if

(6.5)

\#\{x\in T^{+}_{1}:x\leq q-\ell\}+\#\{y\in T^{-}_{1}:y\leq p-\ell\}\leq k-\ell\quad\text{for all $1\leq\ell\leq\min\{k,r\}$};

then by setting
i\coloneqq k-\ell+1
, we rewrite (
6.5
) as

(6.6)

\#\{x\in T^{+}_{1}:x<q-k+i\}+\#\{y\in T^{-}_{1}:y<p-k+i\}<i\quad\text{for all $\max\{0,\>k-r\}<i\leq k$},

which coincides with the necessary and sufficient condition for
T\in\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)
in Definition
3.1
.
Consequently,
{\rm end}(T)\in\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{k}
if and only if
T\in\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)
.
Hence
\coprod_{\mathbf{E}\in\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{E}}(\sigma)=\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)
, and so by (
6.4
) we obtain (
6.1
), which completes the proof for
\U(p,q)
.
∎

Theorem 6.2
.

Assume one of the three settings in Table
2
;
if
G_{\mathbb{R}}=\operatorname{Mp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
, then assume that
k\notin[n+1,\>2n-2]
.
Let
L_{\lambda(\sigma)}
be the simple
\mathfrak{g}
-module with highest weight
\lambda(\sigma)
.
We have

\operatorname{Deg}L_{\lambda(\sigma)}=\#(\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)\times\mathcal{P}_{k}),

with
\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)
and
\mathcal{P}_{k}
as in Definitions
3.1
and
4.1
.
In particular, if
k\geq r
, then
\operatorname{Deg}L_{\lambda(\sigma)}=\#\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)
.

Conjecture 6.3
.

Theorem
6.2
remains true without the assumption on
k
in the
\operatorname{Mp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
case.

Proof of Theorem
6.2
.

Let
G_{\mathbb{R}}=\U(p,q)
or
\operatorname{O}^{*}(2n)
.
First suppose
k<s
.
By Lemma
5.7
we have
\operatorname{Deg}L_{\lambda(\sigma)}=\#\widehat{\mathcal{J}}_{k}(\sigma)
, and so the result follows from the bijection
{\rm id}\times\Theta
in Lemma
6.1
.
In the case
k\geq r
, we have
\#\mathcal{P}_{k}=1
by (
4.1
).

Suppose
k\geq s
.
The
k\geq s
case of Lemma
5.5
, in (
5.7
), states that
L_{\lambda(\sigma)}\cong\bigoplus_{T\in\mathcal{T}(\sigma)}S\,\varphi_{T}
.
In this decomposition, the number of Stanley spaces equals
\#\mathcal{T}(\sigma)
, all of which have the same dimension (i.e., the Krull dimension of
S
, namely
\#\mathbf{P}
).
Thus by Lemma
5.4
, we have
\operatorname{Deg}L_{\lambda(\sigma)}=\#\mathcal{T}(\sigma)
.
Since
k\geq s\geq r
, we have
\mathcal{T}(\sigma)=\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)
by (
3.2
), and
\#\mathcal{P}_{k}=1
by (
4.1
).
This completes the proof for
\U(p,q)
and
\operatorname{O}^{*}(2n)
.

Let
G_{\mathbb{R}}=\Mp(2n,\mathbb{R})
.
Recall from Table
2
that
r=n
.
Thus if
k\leq n
, then by the result (
1.7
) from
[
33
]
, we have

\operatorname{Deg}L_{\lambda(\sigma)}=\dim U_{\sigma}\cdot\deg\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{k}.

But we have
\dim U_{\sigma}=\#\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)
by Lemma
3.4
(a), and
\deg\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{k}=\#\mathcal{P}_{k}
by Proposition
7.6
below.
This completes the proof for the case
k\leq n
.
On the other hand, recall from Table
2
that
s=2n-1
, and now suppose that
k\geq 2n-1
.
Then
L_{\lambda(\sigma)}\cong S\otimes F_{\lambda(\sigma)}
is a free
S
-module, and it follows that
\operatorname{Deg}L_{\lambda(\sigma)}=\dim F_{\lambda(\sigma)}
.
By Lemma
3.4
(b), we have
\dim F_{\lambda(\sigma)}=\#\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)
, and by (
4.1
), we have
\#\mathcal{P}_{k}=1
.
This completes the proof for the case
k\geq 2n-1
.
∎

Note that explicit formulas for the Bernstein degree are obtained by combining Theorem
6.2
with our enumerative Propositions
3.6
and
4.2
.

7.
Open problems and observations

The main result in this paper leads naturally to the following problems.

Problem 7.1
.

Prove Conjecture
6.3
.

From our perspective, the ideal way to solve Problem
7.1
would be to extend Lemma
5.5
to the
G_{\mathbb{R}}=\operatorname{Mp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
case, by defining analogues of jellyfish for
\operatorname{Mp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
.
Note that Conjecture
6.3
at least provides a candidate for the subset of maximal jellyfish, namely
\widehat{\mathcal{J}}_{k}(\sigma)=\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)\times\Theta(\mathcal{P}_{k})
.
We hope that this may ultimately suggest the correct way to define the full set
\mathcal{J}_{k}(\sigma)
of jellyfish, which (upon proof) would then lead to the solution of Problem
7.1
.

Problem 7.2
.

Extend Theorem
6.2
to those unitary highest weight modules of
\operatorname{O}^{*}(2n)
arising outside the dual pair setting.

Unlike the groups
\U(p,q)
and
\operatorname{Mp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
, the group
\operatorname{O}^{*}(2n)
admits unitary highest weight representations arising outside the dual pair setting; that is, certain unitary highest weight modules are not of the form
L_{\lambda(\sigma)}
for some
\sigma\in\widehat{H}(k)
.
For details, see
[
7
]
*pp. 48–49.
Without the group
H(k)
in the picture, Problem
7.2
amounts to determining a set of combinatorial objects, whether tableaux or otherwise, to play the role of
\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)
in Theorem
6.2
.

Solving Problems
7.1
–
7.2
would establish a uniform combinatorial interpretation of the Bernstein degree of
all
unitary highest weight modules for the groups
\U(p,q)
,
\operatorname{Mp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
, and
\operatorname{O}^{*}(2n)
.
The ultimate goal of the program is to extend such an interpretation beyond those groups:

Problem 7.3
.

Extend Theorem
6.2
uniformly to arbitrary unitary highest weight modules (including those of the exceptional groups).

Unlike Problem
7.1
(where for
\operatorname{Mp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
we had already defined
\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)
and
\mathcal{P}_{k}
), and Problem
7.2
(where for
\operatorname{O}^{*}(2n)
we had already defined
\mathcal{P}_{k}
), solving Problem
7.3
requires us to define analogues of both
\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\sigma)
and
\mathcal{P}_{k}
in a uniform manner for Hermitian symmetric pairs of all types.
As a first step toward solving Problem
7.3
, we now give a uniform generalization of the diagram
D_{k}
that yields an analogous set
\mathcal{P}_{k}
for all groups of Hermitian type; see Proposition
7.6
below.

In the following discussion, suppose
G_{\mathbb{R}}
is of Hermitian type, that is, one of the seven groups listed in Table
4
(page
4
).
Recall from (
2.1
) the set
\Phi(\mathfrak{p}^{+})
of positive noncompact roots of the complexified Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
.
This set
\Phi(\mathfrak{p}^{+})
is a graded poset, where the grading is given by the height of the roots in
\Phi(\mathfrak{p}^{+})
.
In the third column of Table
4
, we show the Hasse diagram of
\Phi(\mathfrak{p}^{+})
for the five types in which
\mathfrak{g}
is simply laced.
For the remaining two types,
\mathfrak{g}
is not simply laced:

•

G_{\mathbb{R}}=\operatorname{Mp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
, in which case
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{R}}=\mathfrak{sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
and
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sp}(2n,\mathbb{C})
.

•

G_{\mathbb{R}}=\SO(2,2n-1)
, in which case
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{R}}=\mathfrak{so}(2,2n-1)
and
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{so}(2n+1)
.

In each of these two types, however, there is a certain Hermitian symmetric pair
(\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathbb{R}},\widetilde{\mathfrak{k}}_{\mathbb{R}})
with
\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}}
simply laced, admitting a natural embedding
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{R}}\hookrightarrow\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathbb{R}}
, as shown below (taking
n=7
):

(7.1)

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{R}}&\Phi(\mathfrak{p}^{+})&&\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathbb{R}}&\Phi(\,\widetilde{\mathfrak{p}}^{+})\\[8.61108pt]

{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -31.85 44.54 C -31.85 45.4 -32.55 46.1 -33.41 46.1 C -34.27 46.1 -34.96 45.4 -34.96 44.54 C -34.96 43.68 -34.27 42.98 -33.41 42.98 C -32.55 42.98 -31.85 43.68 -31.85 44.54 Z M -33.41 44.54}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -20.71 44.54 C -20.71 45.4 -21.41 46.1 -22.27 46.1 C -23.13 46.1 -23.83 45.4 -23.83 44.54 C -23.83 43.68 -23.13 42.98 -22.27 42.98 C -21.41 42.98 -20.71 43.68 -20.71 44.54 Z M -22.27 44.54}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{array}

(In terms of Cartan types,
\widetilde{{\rm C}}_{n}={\rm A}_{2n-1}
and
\widetilde{{\rm B}}_{n}={\rm D}_{n+1}
, respectively.)
We observe from (
7.1
) that
\Phi(\,\widetilde{\mathfrak{p}}^{+})
is self-dual as a graded poset, with height
2n-1
.
The dotted line in (
7.1
) indicates the “middle rank,” that is, those roots
\alpha\in\Phi(\,\widetilde{\mathfrak{p}}^{+})
such that
\operatorname{height}(\alpha)=n
.
Define the lower order ideal

(7.2)

\Phi(\,\widetilde{\mathfrak{p}}^{+})_{\leqslant}\coloneqq\{\alpha\in\Phi(\,\widetilde{\mathfrak{p}}^{+}):\operatorname{height}(\alpha)\leq n\},

whose Hasse diagram is the bottom “half” of that of
\Phi(\,\widetilde{\mathfrak{p}}^{+})
; see the third column of Table
4
.

Definition 7.4
.

Let
G_{\mathbb{R}}
be of Hermitian type, with real rank
r
.
Let

D_{0}\coloneqq\text{the $135^{\circ}$ clockwise rotation of the Hasse diagram of }\begin{cases}\Phi(\mathfrak{p}^{+})&\text{if $\mathfrak{g}$ is simply laced},\\
\Phi(\,\widetilde{\mathfrak{p}}^{+})_{\leqslant}&\text{if $\mathfrak{g}$ is not simply laced},\end{cases}

with each point in the Hasse diagram replaced by a box in
D_{0}
.
The
interior
of a diagram
D
is the set of all boxes that have a box of
D
directly to their southwest.
For
1\leq k\leq r
, recursively define

D_{k}\coloneqq\text{interior of $D_{k-1}$}.

In Table
4
(page
4
), we present the case-by-case details from Definition
7.4
.
Note that for
G_{\mathbb{R}}=\U(p,q)
or
\operatorname{Mp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
or
\operatorname{O}^{*}(2n)
, we recover the diagrams
D_{k}
given in Definition
4.1
.
Therefore, the following is a true generalization of Definition
4.1
:

Definition 7.5
.

In the setting of Definition
7.4
, we define

\mathcal{P}_{k}\coloneqq\{\textup{plane partitions bounded by $k$ and contained in $D_{k}$}\}.

For
P\in\mathcal{P}_{k}
, let
P_{ij}
denote its entry in row
i
(from the top) and column
j
(from the left).
If no such entry exists, then set
P_{ij}=0
.
Define the statistic

(7.3)

c(P)\coloneqq\sum_{i,j}\big(P_{ij}-\max\{P_{i+1,j},P_{i,j-1}\}\big),

where the sum ranges over all pairs
(i,j)
corresponding to a box in
D_{k}
.

Proposition 7.6
.

Let
(G_{\mathbb{R}},K_{\mathbb{R}})
be an irreducible Hermitian symmetric pair of noncompact type, and let
r
denote the real rank of
G_{\mathbb{R}}
.
For all
1\leq k\leq r
, we have the Hilbert series

H_{\mathbb{C}[\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{k}]}(t)=\frac{\sum_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{k}}t^{c(P)}}{(1-t)^{\dim\mathfrak{p}^{+}-|D_{k}|}},

and therefore

\#\mathcal{P}_{k}=\deg\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{k}.

Proof.

Observe that the bijection
\Theta:\mathcal{P}_{k}\longrightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{k}
(see Definition
4.4
) generalizes uniformly across all Hermitian symmetric types, where
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{k}
now denotes the set of facets of the
k
th order complex of
\Phi(\mathfrak{p}^{+})
or
\Phi(\,\widetilde{\mathfrak{p}}^{+})_{\leqslant}
.
(From now on, to simplify our descriptions, we will consider the
135^{\circ}
clockwise rotation of this Hasse diagram, meaning that it has the same shape and orientation as the diagram
D_{0}
.
We write
(i,j)
to denote the root in row
i
and column
j
with respect to this orientation.)
Thus by (
4.5
), if
P\in\mathcal{P}_{k}
then
\Theta(P)
is a maximal family of nonintersecting lattice paths
\mathbf{N}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{N}_{k}
in the Hasse diagram of
\Phi(\mathfrak{p}^{+})
or
\Phi(\,\widetilde{\mathfrak{p}}^{+})_{\leqslant}
.
For
\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{N}_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup\mathbf{N}_{k}\in\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{k}
, define its
corners
as follows:

(7.4)

{\rm cor}(\mathbf{F})\coloneqq\coprod_{t=1}^{k}{\rm cor}(\mathbf{N}_{t}),\quad\text{where }{\rm cor}(\mathbf{N_{t}})\coloneqq\{(i,j)\in\mathbf{N}_{t}:(i-1,j),(i,j+1)\in\mathbf{N}_{t}\}.

In other words, a corner of
\mathbf{F}
is a point at which one of its constituent lattice paths
\mathbf{N}_{t}
turns from south to east.
(This is a typical convention in the literature, although it differs from the definition of a corner in our paper
[
14
]
.)
Since all elements of
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{k}
have the same cardinality, we may define

d_{k}\coloneqq\#\mathbf{F}\text{ for any (equivalently, every) }\mathbf{F}\in\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{k},

coinciding with the definition of
d_{k}
in (
5.11
).
We observe from Definition
7.4
that

(7.5)

d_{k}=|D_{0}|-|D_{k}|.

Moreover, we have

(7.6)

\dim\mathfrak{p}^{+}=\#\Phi(\mathfrak{p}^{+})=\begin{cases}2n-1&\text{if $G_{\mathbb{R}}=\SO(2,2n-1)$},\\
|D_{0}|&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

First consider the dual pair setting (i.e., the first three rows of Table
4
).
In this setting, the
K
-orbit closure
\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{k}
is the determinantal variety

(7.7)

\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{k}=\begin{cases}\{X\in{\rm M}_{p,q}:\operatorname{rk}X\leq k\}&\text{if $G_{\mathbb{R}}=\U(p,q)$},\\
\{X\in{\rm SM}_{n}:\operatorname{rk}X\leq k\}&\text{if $G_{\mathbb{R}}=\operatorname{Mp}(2n,\mathbb{R})$},\\
\{X\in{\rm AM}_{n}:\operatorname{rk}X\leq 2k\}&\text{if $G_{\mathbb{R}}=\operatorname{O}^{*}(2n)$},\\
\end{cases}

with notation as in the last column of Table
1
.
Applying the well-known results from the 1990s
[
41
,
3
,
6
,
17
]
on Stanley decompositions and Hilbert series of these determinantal rings, we obtain

(7.8)

H_{\mathbb{C}[\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{k}]}(t)=\frac{\sum_{\mathbf{F}\in\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{k}}t^{\#{\rm cor}(\mathbf{F})}}{(1-t)^{d_{k}}}.

Let
P\in\mathcal{P}_{k}
, and put
\Theta(P)=\mathbf{N}_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup\mathbf{N}_{k}
as in Definition
4.4
.
Recall the subdiagrams
P_{t}
in Definition
4.4
.
A box
(i,j)
in
P_{t}
is said to be an
outer corner
if
P_{t}
contains neither
(i+1,j)
nor
(i,j-1)
.
By the construction of
\Theta
in Definition
4.4
, for all
1\leq t\leq k
there is a bijective correspondence

{\rm cor}(\mathbf{N}_{t})\longleftrightarrow\{\text{outer corners of $P_{t}$}\},

where
{\rm cor}(\mathbf{N}_{t})
was defined in (
7.4
).
Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\#{\rm cor}(\Theta(P))=\sum_{t=1}^{k}\#{\rm cor}(\mathbf{N}_{t})

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{k}\#\{\text{outer corners of $P_{t}$}\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j}\#\{t:\text{$(i,j)$ is an outer corner of $P_{t}$}\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j}\big(P_{ij}-\max\{P_{i+1,j},P_{i,j-1}\}\big)

(7.9)

\displaystyle=c(P).

Thus, starting with (
7.8
) and applying the bijection
\Theta
along with (
7.5
)–(
7.6
) and (
7.9
), we obtain

(7.10)

H_{\mathbb{C}[\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{k}]}(t)=\frac{\sum_{\mathbf{F}\in\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{k}}t^{\#{\rm cor}(\mathbf{F})}}{(1-t)^{d_{k}}}=\frac{\sum_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{k}}t^{\#{\rm cor}(\Theta(P))}}{(1-t)^{|D_{0}|-|D_{k}|}}=\frac{\sum_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{k}}t^{c(P)}}{(1-t)^{\dim\mathfrak{p}^{+}-|D_{k}|}},

which completes the proof in the dual pair setting.

Outside the dual pair setting, with the exception of the case
G_{\mathbb{R}}=\SO(2,2n-1)
, we carried out a case-by-case verification of (
7.8
) in our previous paper
[
14
]
*§7.
We stated this result in
[
14
]
in terms of the
k
th Wallach representation

L_{-kc\zeta}
, whose Hilbert series coincides with that of
\mathbb{C}[\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{k}]
.
Hence for all but
G_{\mathbb{R}}=\SO(2,2n-1)
, the result follows again from (
7.10
).

Finally, let
G_{\mathbb{R}}=\SO(2,2n-1)
.
Since
r=2
, we need only verify the case
k=1
.
As above, the Hilbert series of
\mathbb{C}[\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{1}]
coincides with that of the first Wallach representation of
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{R}}
.
By
[
9
]
*Thm. 27, this Hilbert series is given by

H_{\mathbb{C}[\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{1}]}(t)=\frac{1+t}{(1-t)^{2n-2}}.

Since
D_{1}
is a single box, we have
\mathcal{P}_{1}=\{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to8.4pt{\vss\hbox to8.00006pt{\hss$\scriptstyle 0$\hss}\vss}\kern-8.4pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-8.4pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr}}\kern 345.4pt}}$},\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to8.4pt{\vss\hbox to8.00006pt{\hss$\scriptstyle 1$\hss}\vss}\kern-8.4pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-8.4pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr}}\kern 345.4pt}}$}\}
, where
c(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to8.4pt{\vss\hbox to8.00006pt{\hss$\scriptstyle 0$\hss}\vss}\kern-8.4pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-8.4pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr}}\kern 345.4pt}}$})=0
and
c(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to8.4pt{\vss\hbox to8.00006pt{\hss$\scriptstyle 1$\hss}\vss}\kern-8.4pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-8.4pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr}}\kern 345.4pt}}$})=1
, and we have
\dim\mathfrak{p}^{+}-|D_{k}|=2n-2
by (
7.6
).
This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 7.7
.

Proposition
7.6
provides a combinatorial interpretation for the
Wonderful Correspondence
observed in
[
8
]
*§8.1.
In particular, if
G_{\mathbb{R}}=\U(p,q)
or
\Mp(2n,\mathbb{R})
or
\operatorname{O}^{*}(2n)
, then there is a “reduced” Hermitian symmetric pair
(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{R}},\mathfrak{k}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{R}})
, such that the Hilbert series of
\mathbb{C}[\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{k}]
(equivalently, of the
k
th Wallach representation
L_{-kc\zeta}
of
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{R}}
) can be expressed in rational form as

H_{\mathbb{C}[\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{k}]}(t)=\frac{H_{E}(t)}{(1-t)^{\dim\mathfrak{p}^{+}-\dim\mathfrak{p}^{\prime+}}},

where
E
is the simple finite-dimensional
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
-module with highest weight
k\zeta^{\prime}
.
(Here
\zeta^{\prime}
is the unique fundamental weight of
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
that is orthogonal to the compact roots; for details, see Table 3 on page 178 of
[
8
]
, and Table 3 in
[
15
]
for Types I–IIIa.)
Thus, comparing with Proposition
7.6
, it follows that the Hilbert series of
E
can be understood as the generating function for the
c
statistic on plane partitions, namely

H_{E}(t)=\sum_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{k}}t^{c(P)},

or equivalently, the generating function for the number of turns in maximal families of
k
nonintersecting lattice paths in
\mathbf{P}
.
Moreover, we have
|D_{k}|=\dim\mathfrak{p}^{\prime+}
.
The Wonderful Correspondence is a special case of
Enright–Shelton reduction

[
11
,
13
]
.
In fact, for all seven types in Table
4
, the subdiagram
D_{k}
can be interpreted as the Hasse diagram of
\Phi(\mathfrak{p}^{\prime+})
for a certain pair
(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{R}},\mathfrak{k}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{R}})
obtained via Enright–Shelton reduction.

Beyond the Wallach representations

In Table
3
, we list certain families of unitary highest weight modules for the exceptional groups
\operatorname{E}_{6(-14)}
and
\operatorname{E}_{7(-25)}
, excluding the Wallach representations mentioned above.
For certain families among these modules
L_{\lambda}
, we have observed an analogue of the Nishiyama–Ochiai–Taniguchi result (
1.7
), which we recall below:

(7.11)

\operatorname{Deg}L_{\lambda}=\dim U_{\sigma}\cdot\deg\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{k},

where

k
is the positive integer such that
\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{k}=\mathcal{AV}(L_{\lambda})
, and
U_{\sigma}
is the irreducible representation of the group
H_{\mathbb{R}}(k)
(corresponding to
G_{\mathbb{R}}
via Howe duality) with highest weight
\sigma
.
By an analogue of this result for
G_{\mathbb{R}}=\operatorname{E}_{6(-14)}
or
\operatorname{E}_{7(-25)}
, we mean a family of unitary highest weight modules
L_{\lambda}
for
G_{\mathbb{R}}
, along with a corresponding group
H_{\mathbb{R}}(k)
, and a correspondence
\lambda\mapsto\sigma
that satisfies (
7.11
).
In this sense, the findings in Table
3
suggest an analogue of the dual reductive pairs
(G_{\mathbb{R}},H_{\mathbb{R}}(k))
arising from Howe duality.
The one-parameter family for
(\operatorname{E}_{7},\operatorname{B}_{4})
was observed by Kato–Ochiai
[
29
]
; see their Proposition 5.2 and Remark 5.5.
However, the family for
\operatorname{E}_{6}
and the two-parameter family for
(\operatorname{E}_{7},\operatorname{F}_{4})
seem to be new.

,

Table 4
.
The diagram
D_{k}
in Definition
7.4
.
In the two non-simply laced cases, namely
\operatorname{Mp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
and
\SO(2,2n-1)
, the third column shows the Hasse diagram of
\Phi(\,\widetilde{\mathfrak{p}}^{+})_{\leqslant}
defined in (
7.2
).
For our diagrams we take
n=10
, with
k=3
in the first three rows.
Note that
D_{r}=\varnothing
in all types.
